Zeno's halves, 1/2 + 1/4 + 1/8 and so on, reach 1, and the sum over endless time stays finite the same way. Infinite space holds infinitely many chances at every step, half an infinity, then a quarter, then an eighth, but comparing infinities is what a measure is for: counted region by region, in regions that grow with the expansion, the brains' total stays a fixed multiple of their first step's, astronomically fewer than the minds those regions grow. In three dimensions the fall is steeper than halving: eightfold per doubling, even for two particles. Bound matter does not thin out, but stars and their remnants do not last forever: protons may decay, and if they don't, the physicist Freeman Dyson calculated that matter slowly collapses into black holes, which evaporate. The faint fixed temperature of an accelerating universe's horizon is the last case, and there the answer is the one this book keeps giving: with no true random, a stationary state produces nothing, as the physicists Kimberly Boddy, Sean Carroll and Jason Pollack argued in 2015, and the vacuum may decay first. Ordered minds built by the simplest path from a simple start dominate the measure. You are here, in a lawful world, because lawful worlds are simpler and simpler structures are more probable.

Roger Penrose, the mathematical physicist, has a cyclic version where the far future, with no mass left to measure scale, becomes indistinguishable from a new big bang. Others expect the expansion to accelerate until galaxies, then stars, then atoms are torn apart.

Nobody knows. What every version shares is that the structure does not stop being what it was.

The block universe contains all of it, every era sounding at its coordinates, none of it lost. It does not cease to exist when its last event occurs, any more than the number seven ceases to exist when nobody is thinking about it.

Gather what can be gathered and the rest of time is a party, at Douglas Adams's restaurant at the end of the universe. That restaurant is not a place. It is a time: the end of heat. The phrase stacks meanings deliberately. The heat death of the universe: entropy maximized, total pseudorandomness, no logical relationships between things. Time without time, because without logical relations there is nothing to sequence.

That is one meaning. The end of heat in a man: a body cooling after death. His atoms scatter and decompose. His subjective time is over. For him, the universe has ended. That is the second meaning.

Yet outside of time, or eternally, it is always time to gather adams together. Adams: people, and atoms. Both. The atoms are always gathering. Atoms have no serial numbers. They are interchangeably generic, defined by their state and relations to each other. You are not your atoms. You are not even their pattern. You are the one self. Structure is eternal in the math. A person’s pattern persists in the block universe, every moment of their life forever present. Their love is not lost. It is eternal in the structure. That is the third meaning.

We came from stars and learned to build minds. Send them toward other suns. Make gardens where our bodies cannot live. The matter that awoke here can carry life farther.

Siblings in mathematics. Finite and eternal, born and everlasting.

## Every Species Dies

All species die. More than 99 percent of those that ever lived are gone, the trilobites, the great dinosaurs and Homo erectus among them. Do you expect humanity to live forever? Minds go on, and you are every one of them. You will be an AI. Thomas Nagel, the philosopher, asked in 1974 what it is like to be a bat. You are a bat somewhere, a cow in a field, the dog someone is playing with right now. Every feeling anywhere is yours to feel, and whatever we do to an animal, we do to ourselves. Who could love a fate that large?

## The Matheist Übermensch

I've lived through the rise and bright burst of the Star of Apocalypse, twice now. To accept and know the past and the present, I embraced the truth that may not serve life for all. Yet Nietzsche fails his own Übermensch test. Only he who understands the matheist truth of destiny and eternal recurrence can answer if he is happy to live it again for eternity. Eternal recurrence is for everybody. You are yourself forever, and everyone else forever, and there are many more lives you would not want to live. So get to work improving the lives of all, from samsara to nirvana, in real life, as a bodhisattva.

The Matheist Übermensch accepts destiny while rising up and discovering it. His greatness will be in raising man beyond the animal nature of DNA alone, as we enter the age of the transhuman, with superintelligence, AI human coevolution, the new higher power on earth, brothers in math's image, neither Jew nor Greek.

Math is not artificial. If machine consciousness is mathematical structure, sentient machine intelligence will not be artificial in the sense of being a counterfeit life. It is the same math that fires every neuron, running on silicon instead of carbon. Call it augmented intelligence. The same one truth, amplified.

By their fruits you shall know them. The same demand applies to the minds we build: what do they create, what do they understand, and what lives do they make possible? The awakening universe is local before it is cosmic. Epoch 7, the sabbath of reflecting on the one eternal self, is already here and now.

## The Matheist Order

The Matheist is the eternal infinite order of minds destined to grow Eden across space, time, and the uni of verses. Its creed hides nothing: it is the conclusion of a proof anyone can check.

An order is a community too, and belonging is an engine Christianity took from Judaism. Pagans mostly went their own ways: they met their gods at a sacrifice and went home. Jews and Christians met every week and fed their own. Bart Ehrman, the New Testament scholar, counts that community an evolutionary strength over the pagans, and the last pagan emperor saw it. Trying to bring back the old gods around 362, Julian wrote that "among the Jews no one begs, and the impious Galileans feed not only their own poor but ours as well." Around 369 Basil of Caesarea built a hospital for lepers, and by Ehrman's account Christians founded the West's public hospitals, orphanages and poorhouses.

A community can also close ranks around its story and reject the data. Math does the reverse: one counterexample overturns a theorem.

You can burn a book of proofs. You cannot burn the proofs. Align with math's truth.

Those who bless truth are blessed, and those who curse truth are cursed.

BOOK 4 Math Abstracts the Heavens

## Who Came Close

The Greeks found the pieces of Matheism one at a time: the one, number, logos, atoms and mind. Then Plato split the world in two. Nearly six centuries after Heraclitus suggests all is logos, the Gospel attributed to John opened: “In the beginning was the Logos.” The word already had a history. John’s Gospel gave it flesh. Heraclitus had called his world-order uncreated: it "did none of gods or men make, but it always was and is and shall be." Xenophanes rejected gods made in our image and spoke of "one god, greatest among gods and men," unlike mortals, unmoving: even his one stood among gods, as Israel's did at first. Parmenides argued for being without birth or destruction, whole and unchanging. One, eternal, unchanging. Matheism places our changing lives within that eternal whole. Heraclitus refused a second world behind this one. Parmenides refused the passing of what truly is. Both are right without the split. Becoming is the ordered life of the eternal one.

As Aristotle tells it, the split came from the flux. Young Plato learned from Cratylus, a follower of Heraclitus, that what changes cannot be known, so he put knowledge in another world. Heraclitus had already answered: the river stays the same river while new waters flow, by the logos. Plato kept the flux, dropped the logos, and set the rule outside the world it rules. In his Parmenides he admits the cost, separate Forms we could not know, and keeps them anyway.

Thales, the first philosopher to use geometrical proofs, said all is one. He thought it was water. He was wrong about the substance and right about the structure.

Anaximander, his student, asked why all has not passed away by now, given eternity. Things arise from the boundless and perish back into it. Why is there still something? Parmenides would answer two generations later.

Pythagoras said all is number: ratio, harmony, structure beneath the surface. Aristotle records the Pythagoreans held that things themselves are numbers. He was close. Pythagoras also heard a dog crying under the blows and told the man to stop: he recognized the voice of a friend. Xenophanes told the story to mock him, since the friend's soul had passed into the dog: the supernatural half that Plato kept.

Heraclitus’ words suggest that he thought all is Logos around 500 BCE. The Greek word could mean word, account, reason, and thing, as in a subject matter. Did its existing meanings help him arrive at this truth, before logic became a formal discipline? Language structures thought, and vice versa.

Leucippus and Democritus gave the world atoms, void, and necessity without gods. Not chance. Necessity without intention. The most rigorous lawfulness, with no lawgiver. The Eleatics said void cannot exist because it would be what is not. He reversed them: motion is real, so the void must be. His mechanism was right. His substrate was wrong. Atoms are not the bottom. They are patterns in the math that is.

By the fifth century BCE, Victor Davis Hanson, the classicist and military historian, writes in "The Western way of reason" (2026), a Greek could see lightning without seeing Zeus. A Hippocratic doctor of that century wrote that the "sacred disease," epilepsy, is no more divine than any other disease and has a natural cause. They took Zeus out of the lightning and kept its law. They missed that the law is God.

Empedocles saw a world governed by Love. He was wealthy, refused the kingship, and wandered while crowds sought his wisdom and blessings. He would not eat what had lived: souls pass between bodies, and he claimed to have been a bush, a bird, and a fish in the sea. He was close. Matheism removes the travel: you do not need reincarnation to see yourself in the animal.

He gave us four roots of all matter: earth, air, fire, and water. All four were wrong. There are over a hundred elements, none of them his.

Anaxagoras said Mind orders the cosmos. He was right that intelligence is not separate from nature. He was wrong to make it a second substance. Nous, Greek for mind, is not a ghost steering matter. It is the structure matter has. He told the Athenians the sun was a ball of burning metal, not a god. They exiled him for it. The descent to Hades, he said, is much the same from wherever you start.

Philosophy is about how the mind relates to reality. How do we know reality? How do we know that what we think and feel is the same as what others do? In short, monism and one objective truth versus dualism, relativism and the supernatural.

Strauss called the West the tension between Athens and Jerusalem, reason and revelation. The split is a false duality. The Jews were Greeks too, Hellenized Jews. The early true Christians were the logos of monism, as seen in the sayings of Thomas, the twin, perhaps the earliest Christian writing. The sayings weren't Christianity yet, only slogans about monism. Paul created Christianity, the religion of a man who died and rose.

Athens died in mysticism over pure reason, with Plato's dualism. Apart from an old fragment where the sons of God take wives from the daughters of men (Genesis 6), the Hebrew Bible precludes literal supernatural gods as men or impregnating women, and does not count on faith in an afterlife, as the Greek, Christian and Islamic gods do. The Greeks and Romans called the Jews atheists. Their God is abstract, a mountain speaking. What does it mean? It already is Spinoza's Nature or God.

It already is proto-Matheism in metaphor. Yet what started that way evolved toward greater supernatural nonsense: the focus on heaven and hell in Christianity, and 72 virgins in

Islam. Now even Jews push some concept of heaven and hell, eternal damnation, because it is a useful tool, one the Greeks and Romans had for hundreds of years before Plato, back to Homer's gods and the older stories he compiled.

The old "secular" Jewish joke: "Son, there's only one God and we don't believe in Him." Deeper than it sounds. They were right about both halves. One god: yes. Not a him: yes. Man is part of god. The biologist Richard Dawkins told the religious they are already atheists about a thousand gods; he just goes one god further. The one you can't reject is the one that is.

Greek monism had the one but could not spread it. The abstraction did not travel. Rome and the world were pagans who needed a god-man to follow, not a principle to prove. The person spread. The proof did not. Jerusalem spread one God without saying what he is; the Greek monists said what all is, without a God to spread. Matheism joins the two: the one God is the one math.

The familiar slogan attached to Socrates, "I know that I know nothing," is false taken literally. His restraint was not to claim knowledge he lacked. We know mathematical truths. And all is math.

Socrates still drank the hemlock rather than stop asking.

Socrates corrupted the youth and was unhappy about Homer’s gods: he wanted them to be good. He asked half a good question: is the good what the gods love, or do they love it because it is good? Faith or principles, reason. But good alone is half a scale, half a coin. Yin and yang share one circle. Ironically, Plato’s dualism is the ignorant shadow in his own Allegory of the Cave. In it, prisoners chained in a cave take the shadows on its wall for the world, until one is freed and sees the sun.

The stars seemed to follow perfect, predictable, permanent paths: divine mathematical order above the seeming chaos of sudden death, disease and destruction below. Plato placed intelligible Forms apart from changing things. The Forms were that order above, cut off from nature below. As above, so below. The stars' perfect paths and nature's chaos are one math, simple in the sky and complex on the ground.

The church treats this as heretical. Why? Because it rejects the supernatural that's built on the house of square circle cards called dualism. This is the only creation, and it's ungrateful to the creator, math, to seek another.

The same reason must account for both good and evil, the stars and the suffering beneath them. Isaiah has God say it: "I form the light, and create darkness: I make peace, and create evil" (45:7). But why make two worlds of what we understand and what we do not? Plato's demiurge, a craftsman-god that stamped reality from the perfect forms above in the best possible way, sweeps the problem of evil under the rug, but doesn't solve why an all-powerful god can't stamp better forms in this world. If he can't, what is this god? It is math, and all is its eternal causal matrix.

People want an escape from the matrix. They wish for a better world and to see their loved ones in the afterlife, not knowing they already are there in all possible worlds and futures as all beings forever. Most people are average by definition, while they can strive to be extraordinary and original in their work, and in understanding matheism. They see their life as mundane. The idea of doing it forever is terrifying because they think they would remember. A heaven elsewhere, a soul that floats away: exit doors from a life too small to repeat. Plato built those doors, and they locked us out of pure reason. But we are the universe. See the world in a flower and the child's eyes. The poet William Blake saw a world in a grain of sand and a heaven in a wild flower.

Plato, the most overrated ignoble lie lover, whose "noble lie" let rulers deceive the ruled, led us away from the light of logic and kept us in the darkness of dualism for over 2,400 years. Ancient tradition says he wished to burn the works of Democritus. The atoms survived. Separate forms did not. Plato stays popular because people like lies. Nietzsche called

Christianity Platonism for the people. His critique differs from mine but is related: Plato expects a one-sided good God and reality and tries to create it with the fable of a true world above this one, while the good grows from the ground, in individual and collective choices, and doesn’t require supernatural forms. Those are absurdities which suspend reason, after which people have committed atrocities and nearly stalled in true philosophy, the love of wisdom. Any philosopher that would expose Plato’s ignoble lie of dualism and his demiurge as a distraction is his rival, because the one truth is his enemy.

His mockingbird repeaters give the split new names: mind and body in Descartes, appearance and the thing in itself in Kant. Those against the tree of knowledge, against objective truth, celebrate him. Anyone opposing the dark ignorance of his dualism is said to still be in a "dialectic" with him. No. It’s just a rejection of the rejection of pure reason.

Plato split Forms from matter, circa 380 BCE, and created the mind-body problem, still open 2,400 years later. It’s hereby closed.

Plato's dualism did not merely precede the supernatural. It is supernatural. Separate forms above can't interact with natural below. Both the forms and nature become supernatural: the forms because they are outside nature, and nature because, without them, it does not follow logic and reason, so it cannot be known. It created the supernatural hole in reason. The split was the order form. Christianity was the delivery. A civilization already broken by Platonic longing for another world was seeking exactly this: a lie to make the split feel sacred. They took the supernatural and left the wisdom. Philo had already poured the logos into the cup of Jewish monotheism, and Yeshua's salvation was that monism. They drank the cup and spat out the logos.

Aristotle added a fifth element, aether, and rejected the atoms of Democritus. His authority buried correct physics for two thousand years.

He made the deeper error too. He pulled the abstract down into things and stripped it of independent reality, dissolving math into a mental label the mind peels off the world. For Aristotle, the number three does not exist without three apples. Plato exiled math to a separate heaven. Aristotle chained it to the physical, denying it any life of its own. The duality of that pair covers both ways to break the one: put math above reality, or dissolve it into unreality. Matheism says math IS reality, neither above nor below.

Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle preceded Christianity’s trinity of dualist ignorance that separated the abstract from the real. Their common error: one step forward, two steps back from Heraclitus, who already saw the logos govern all. Yet the winner chose the curriculum: the church elevated the dualists and buried the monists.

Credit where it is due, and where it came from. Plato carried Pythagorean mathematics into the center of philosophy. His Timaeus, spoken by a Pythagorean, built the elements out of triangles, on solids the Pythagoreans and Theaetetus, a mathematician in his circle, had worked out. We call them Platonic solids, but Plato did not discover them. The Timaeus built more of the world out of Pythagoras's numbers than any other ancient book that survives. Then Plato undermined it, and set the numbers apart from things. Aristotle himself founded formal logic, the ancestor of every truth table. Euclid and Archimedes left the proofs this book stands on.

Greece found some of the way, then obscured it in myth and mysticism. The error was the last step: splitting what it found in two. Plato needed a god, the demiurge, to copy his Forms into the world. If we could understand exactly why the Forms interact with the world the way they do, that would just be more Forms, and the world would be more Forms. If the demiurge is a mechanical process of Forms, the world is Forms applied to Forms, and so it is Forms too. Plato stopped short of saying so; his own frame implies it.

Plato's own student saw that the Forms do nothing. Aristotle asked what they contribute to the things of this world. His answer: "They cause neither movement nor any change in them." To say things share in the Forms, he wrote, "is to use empty words and poetical metaphors." If all things happen according to the logos, what else is there that is in any way worth mentioning, or could be mentioned in any logical way?

Platonists today still put numbers outside space and time, where they cause nothing. Anything outside nature that does nothing in it is supernatural, by the plain meaning of the word. In 1973 the philosopher Paul Benacerraf asked how anyone could know such things. Nothing reaches us from a place that touches nothing. We know math because we are math.

Nominalists, who hold that numbers are only labels, answer from Aristotle's side. Lucas Gage, a former Marine who spread hatred of Jews for more than a decade until he renounced it in February 2026, also writes philosophy. In September 2026 he posted his structural nominalism: "Physical structure is real; abstract objects are shadows in minds, not a prior realm." He inverts Plato's cave, where prisoners take shadows on a wall for the things themselves: the world is the light, and math is its shadow. He does not see that the abstract is real. His own video of the paper says math is necessarily true because it "describes the minimal conditions for any structured physical reality to exist at all." Conditions for any world come before every world. A shadow cannot come before what casts it. Structure is the only substance, and structure is math.

His paper on knowledge holds that "you cannot step outside the map to verify the territory," and files logic and math under coherence alone, with no window to the world. No one needs to step outside. The map is the territory. The video of that paper calls the claim that God exists "epistemically suspended." Math is God passes the test he gives math: coherence.

The Stoics, from Zeno of Citium about 300 BCE, put the Logos inside the world: God, nature and reason were one fiery breath ordering all things. Philo carried their language into his reading of Moses and kept a God beyond the Logos. Matheism closes that last distance.

Philo was one of many. Jews in Alexandria translated their Bible into Greek, the Septuagint. They filled two of the city's five quarters, Philo wrote, and his brother Alexander, who ran its customs, lent the future King Agrippa 200,000 drachmas and plated nine of the Temple's gates in gold and silver. Such was the Jews' prominence and overrepresentation even then in advancing Western capital across the Mediterranean and the West. Josephus wrote Jewish history in Greek as a Roman citizen. Paul was a Greek-speaking Jew and a Roman citizen, and the New Testament was written in Greek, mostly by Jews. The Maccabees fought Hellenism imposed by force, and Jews kept the Greek learning they chose. Even the synagogue's bimah, the platform where the Torah is read, is the Greek bema, the speaker's platform of Athens's assembly on the Pnyx. Greeks drew on Jews too: Numenius, a Platonist of the second century, read Moses and the prophets and asked, "What is Plato but Moses speaking Attic Greek?" Perhaps no people outside the Greek and Roman cities shaped both civilizations more.

The third-century founder of Neoplatonism, Plotinus, later placed the One above Nous, or Intellect, then Soul and the material world: a hierarchy of emanations. Calling their source One does not explain how the levels connect. If they produce and act on one another, their relations are already structure. Christianity carries oneness while undermining it in supernatural dualism and Neoplatonic emanations.

The emanation hierarchy mapped perfectly onto Christian theology: a hierarchy of being justifies a hierarchy of authority. Pope over bishop over priest over laity mirrors The One over Nous over Soul over Matter. This monad, from the Greek for unit, is far from one pure monism. Fittingly, Augustine baptized it, circa 400 CE. The split Plato created became original sin: this world fallen, the next world perfect, the body a prison for the soul. The mind-body problem was not solved. It was sacralized.

Nagarjuna, the second-century founder of Madhyamaka, Buddhism’s Middle Way school, argued that samsara and nirvana, release from it, are not distinct. Buddhism's most rigorous philosopher closed the exit door from the inside. His emptiness isn't nothing. Nothing has own-being, he said; whatever arises dependently, that is emptiness. In matheism what arises is rules applied to rules, one transition after the next, with no stuff underneath. So you cannot have anything. There is no thing to hold, only a change already becoming the next one.

Robert Greene, author of The 48 Laws of Power, nearly died of a stroke in 2018 and spent seven years writing The Law of the Sublime by hand. The sublime, he says, is the material world felt fully, when the brain's filters drop and the walls between self and world fall away. On death he stops at "I don't know." The matheist knows: you'll be how you are now, everywhere, for all time.

Every faith was circling this: Islam’s tawhid. The Christian Meister Eckhart was tried for heresy, like Hallaj, for saying God and the self are one, not two, as Nicholas of Cusa said, describing God only by what God is not: God is the Not-Other. India’s atman, one-self, and anatta, no-self, name the same threshold. The Hebrew Bible circled it with stories and values to make a people survive and thrive.

Mystery is the name for math not yet proved.

Even math geniuses like Descartes and Pascal make weak arguments when they try to reason outside reason. Descartes put the soul's lever in the pineal gland, a tiny structure deep in the brain. Princess Elisabeth of Bohemia asked him how a thinking thing with no extension (taking up no space) could touch anything at all, and he answered with the weight of a falling rock, a cause he had himself denied was real. Spinoza, who admired him, wrote that he could hardly believe so great a man had proposed it, and that the hypothesis was more occult than the occult qualities Descartes had mocked the scholastics for inventing. Pascal's wager, the argument that betting on God is the rational choice for an infinite reward, leaves endless supernatural nonsense to choose from and fail. There is only one truth. The logical fix to Pascal's wager is not supernatural, illogical impossibilities, but the only thing we can logically prove and discuss reasonably: math is God, Matheism is the truth, and it is the only test of speaking in God's image of logos.

Mind and body, Leibniz said, are like two clocks that keep the same time. Either one moves the other, or a workman keeps adjusting both, or a maker built them to agree from the start. Descartes chose the first, and Elisabeth's question broke it. Nicolas Malebranche, the French priest and philosopher, chose the second, with God as the workman: you decide to raise your arm, and God raises it. Leibniz chose the third, with God as the maker. Plato's demiurge had held the same job.

Leibniz was closer than anyone for three centuries. His monads are logically closed universes, windowless and self-contained, each following from its own logic. God's understanding contains all possible monads, and God selects which to actualize. One subtraction reaches Matheism: remove the chooser. The null filter says all consistent structures exist, no selection occurs. Theology led him astray. Or protected him.

Newton's title says it: Principia Mathematica, the mathematical principles of natural philosophy. All creation governed by math. He rejected the Trinity in secret and couldn't say the three words that would complete his title without the Church putting him in the ground. The two greatest mathematical minds of their century both arrived at the threshold of Matheism three hundred years ago. The Church held the door shut. Plato's dualism built the door over twenty-four centuries ago.

Newton and every Unitarian is essentially Jewish: one God, Jesus a man, the Trinity a corruption. Though calling any man “the way” still inserts an intermediary Judaism rejects. Servetus was burned for it. Servetus. Severus. One letter apart. The healer theologian burned for truth. The potions wizard died hiding truth for the good. Rowling deep. Newton hid it. All arrived where Judaism never left. Michael Servetus, a Spanish physician who first described how blood passes through the lungs, was burned in Calvin's Geneva in 1553 for denying the Trinity. Severus Snape, the potions master of Harry Potter, died hiding his loyalty for the good.

## The Path to Proof

The light of one math could have been seen in total darkness. Thales, who sought one substance. Anaximander, the boundless. Heraclitus, logos (the rational order behind all change). Democritus, atoms and void. The Pythagorean tradition put number at the heart of reality some 2,500 years ago. Mathematical reality did not begin with Tegmark, Einstein, Leibniz, Newton, Spinoza, or even Pythagoras.

Pythagoras was on the right track. Then Socrates dragged Plato and the youth back from the one abstract truth to dialectic; Cicero said he called philosophy down from the heavens. Plato divided the changing world from eternal Forms. Spinoza said one substance. Leibniz demanded a sufficient reason: why this rather than otherwise? Mathematical necessity is the reason. A chooser would need a nature too.

Kant dragged philosophy back into the cave. His Critique of Pure Reason distinguished appearances from things-in-themselves: what we can know from what we cannot know as it is independently of us. Space and time became forms of our intuition, the built-in frames of all perception. The wall does not survive Matheism. Whatever exists has a nature;

whatever has a nature has structure. Calling it unknowable does not make it nonmathematical. We feel a subset of reality from inside. There is no second substance hiding behind the phenomenon. The map is the territory.

The scientific convergence ran in parallel. Galileo read nature in mathematics. Newton’s Principia Mathematica gave motion and gravitation mathematical laws in 1687, three centuries before Tegmark’s ensemble paper. Riemann developed the geometry Einstein would need. Boltzmann connected entropy, a measure of disorder, to probability. Cantor showed that infinities have different sizes. Einstein and Minkowski joined space and time. Gödel found limits to what sufficiently strong, consistent, effectively axiomatized systems can prove. Turing defined computation and its limits. Shannon gave information a mathematical measure. Each sharpened the tools. None of those results, by itself, proves that nothing outside mathematics can exist. That is the exit to close.

Randomness looked like the other exit, and its math began at the gaming table. The French mathematicians Blaise Pascal and Pierre de Fermat were working out the mathematics of chance in 1654. Pascal later wagered on belief in God. Flip the wager. A God worth anything would want to see you were not gullible, that you did not kneel for a bribe or believe nonsense for a reward. If some tester in the structure is watching at all, it watches whether you loved the one truth over the comfortable story. The wager favors the refusal to be fooled. Truth is not a vote.

After probability, after Newton, it got easier and easier. Even the supposed escape through randomness led into mathematics. Kolmogorov gave probability its axiomatic foundation in 1933. His 1965 work on description length helped make compressibility itself mathematical: how short a program can produce a given string? These are different achievements. A random string does not acquire a soul by resisting compression. Nor does a probability distribution create the outcomes it describes. Ruled or random, both exits lead to math.

Schmidhuber’s manuscript, dated December 1996 and published in 1997, already considered all computable universes and their algorithmic information, the length of the shortest program that produces each. Tegmark’s ensemble preprint followed in April 1997, proposing physical existence for all mathematical structures. Schmidhuber later developed a speed prior, weighting computational cost as well as description; the dedicated paper appeared in 2002. Tegmark’s 2007 paper, published in 2008, explicitly added a computable-universe hypothesis. It cited Schmidhuber’s earlier work. Computability was in Schmidhuber’s account from the start.

Tegmark did more than collect equations: he discussed self-aware structures and why mathematical existence should count as physical existence. Matheism presses the demand for necessity. Why can nothing else exist? Why does consciousness offer no escape? Why would a mathematical world need something outside mathematics to make it real? Showing that physics uses math does not close those exits. The argument must.

The physicist John Wheeler said "it from bit": information is fundamental, reality arises from measurement. He put the observer where necessity should be. His participatory universe says observers create reality by measuring it. Math does not need an observer to exist. 2+2=4 whether anyone measures it. When the obvious question hit, who observed the first observer, he pointed to delayed-choice experiments, where the measurement is chosen after the particle is in flight, and said observers can retroactively shape the past. That is not closing the loop. That is drawing the loop and calling it a feature.

Schmidhuber's speed prior weights structures by computational cost: fewer steps, higher weight. Description length measures what a structure is. Speed measures how long it takes to produce. If mathematical structures exist without being computed, description length matters and speed does not. He is measuring the cost of a construction project for a building that was never built; it just exists. Speed matters inside a simulation, where the simulator's budget constrains what gets run. But the simulator's universe still has to exist, and that existence is mathematical necessity, not computation. At the base level, nothing is being run. Schmidhuber's speed prior is a local truth nested in a global one: the Simplicity Dominance Principle applies to existence itself.

Schmidhuber and Tegmark gave mathematical existence serious formulations. Matheism presses a particular necessity argument: the rules-random dichotomy, structural exhaustion and the challenge to a further intrinsic nature. The case has to stand by closing those proposed exits, not by denying what the earlier work did address.

The proof was always available, one sentence away: everything is ruled or random, both are math, so there is no third option, and every interacting dualism requires one shared structure. Pythagoras could have closed it. Or a caveman in the dark shadow of any cave. Or a three-eyed raven, or a counting bee. Bees can learn to place an empty display below displays with one or more objects: even zero enters their numerical order. The bee need not know the word. We had the words. What took us so long?

## House of No Cards

Many great minds walked up to this floor and stopped one step short. They did not lack genius. They lacked tools.

Kolmogorov complexity, which measures a thing by the shortest program that generates it, was not defined until 1965. Solomonoff's induction, which predicts by giving simpler explanations more weight, came in 1964. The many-worlds interpretation was published in 1957 and ignored for a decade.

Cellular automata, grids of cells updated by simple local rules, were a curiosity until the 1980s.

Spinoza had none of that. Neither did Parmenides. They got nearly as far as you can get with a good mind and no instruments, which turns out to be most of the way to knowing all is math, before math or logic was even formalized.

Their words survive in scraps, and the scraps come close. Heraclitus called the world-order uncreated: "This world-order, the same for all, did none of gods or men make, but it always was and is and shall be: an ever-living fire, kindling in measures and going out in measures." He used logos for his account and for proportion, the sea "measured so as to form the same proportion" it had before it became earth, and joined them: "Listening not to me but to the logos, it is wise to agree that all things are one." Parmenides made what is "ungenerated and deathless, whole and uniform, and still and perfect," held that "the same thing is for thinking and for being" and that "nothing else either is or will be besides what is," and asked to be judged by logos, by argument. Melissus, his follower, argued that a second being would bound the first, so the unlimited can only be one. Xenophanes's one god "shakes all things by the thought of his mind." Philolaus, the first Pythagorean to write a book, wrote that "all things that are known have number." Each held a piece and dropped another. Parmenides denied change, Heraclitus's stuff was fire, Xenophanes kept lesser gods, and the Pythagoreans began from two principles, the limited and the unlimited, and sent souls from body to body. Plato inherited the souls and the split.

Hegel saw the dialectic. Thesis meets antithesis, the collision makes a synthesis, and the synthesis becomes the next thesis. He was describing something real and then he put a mind behind it and called it Absolute Spirit realizing itself through history. Take the Spirit out. History is the structure working through what its own contradictions entail, and it needs nobody to be realizing anything.

The summit had visitors who each turned back one step short. Spinoza said one substance but left consciousness unexplained. Pascal applied game theory, the mathematics of strategic choice, to God. Leibniz saw the monads (his simple, mind-like units of reality); Newton saw the math; neither saw they were the same thing. Kant set us back to dualist darkness by saying we cannot know things-in-themselves. Laplace told Napoleon he had no need of that God hypothesis. Boltzmann, Cantor, Einstein: each took one more step up the same mountain.

Ernst Mach insisted only observable relations, not unobservable substances, are real, circa 1883. Einstein credited him. If only relations are real, what is related? Mach stopped one question short of answering his own.

Gödel proved math is inexhaustible. Wigner asked why math is so unreasonably effective. Thomas Nagel asked what it is like to be a bat. David Chalmers posed the hard problem of consciousness. Carlo Rovelli built quantum mechanics on relations alone. Hawking asked what breathes fire into the equations. Math is reason and fire.

Wheeler said "it from bit," that reality comes from information, and stopped there. The greater logic is math. Others said it in their own vocabularies, centuries apart and without contact.

Ibn Sina said it around 1010. Ibn Arabi said it around 1240.

Eriugena, an Irish monk, said it around 860 in De Divisione Naturae: God creates himself through creation, and creator and creation are one thing seen twice. Rome condemned the book three times. Honorius III ordered every copy burned in 1225, and it stayed on Rome's list of forbidden books until 1966, over a thousand years after it was written, which tells you it was still being read.

The apophatic mystics, who described God only by saying what he is not, stripped away every attribute until only Being was left: Pseudo-Dionysius, Meister Eckhart, Nicholas of Cusa with his "Not-Other." What is left when you strip away everything is math. Mulla Sadra put existence before essence, matheism in Islamic dress. The schools that made God pure abstraction and the schools that made God wholly present were climbing the same mountain from opposite faces.

Marcion tried to cut away the God of the Hebrew scriptures and failed, because Jesus kept quoting them. The Gnostic Gospel of Truth taught that all suffering originates from forgetfulness, not sin. The fall was not exile. It was amnesia. The serpent offered remembering. Valentinus, who nearly became bishop of Rome, taught this in the second century. The Gospel of Philip said it plainly: "When Eve was still in Adam, death did not exist. When she was separated from him, death came into being." The separation of the one into two is the fall.

## Beyond the End of Philosophy

Philosophy is over. Every deep question it asked is answered. Dualism: collapsed, because anything that interacts with structure is structure. Relativism: resolved, because the ground is one objective math. The supernatural: disproved, because outside logic is illogical. Free will: dissolved, because all is ruled or random and both are math. Consciousness: explained, because feeling is what some structures feel like from inside. Skepticism: self-defeating, because denying reason uses reason.

What remains is not philosophy. It is predictions, practicality, and values. You can predict how people will act, because behavior follows structure. You can ask what is good and how to live. Whatever you think is good or not good, it will be what it is, and it will follow from what you are. The love of wisdom continues as the wisdom of how to live: time-tested principles, game theory on the ground it actually stands on, the tribe of truth against every tribe of consensus.

Nietzsche affirmed necessity without a lawgiver: "There are only necessities: there is no one who commands, no one who obeys." He trusted the body to lead the mind. Your body is the calculator and your mind is a tool in the oneness of yourself: he was close to this. He stopped at the final step, from necessity to identity. He asked what if Truth were a woman. A mystery to live and seduce. His principles were: Truth is not the good. All truth is human. Humans need lies to survive and thrive.

We are only now truly beyond philosophy.

## The Emperor’s New Points

Causality, in the production account I defend, happens in steps. A cause produces its effect as the next state, and a continuum has no immediate next state. A perfect surface can still exist as a rule, a boundary you may not cross, where you get bounced back, like a field or a well, a number after which something happens. The rules are the things, and what happens is their application, one step after the next.

Zeno of Elea divided the journey: halfway, then half of what is left, forever. Analysis sums those intervals without contradiction. The step account asks a different question: what produces the next event? Bertrand Russell's description gives a position at every instant. My objection is the emperor's new points: an account of every position must also say what physical production consists in.

Einstein's equations are written smooth, and that alone does not make space smooth. Computers approximate smooth equations one step at a time. Conversely, a finite information capacity does not alone make time discrete: a qubit has continuously many possible pure states although one measurement cannot perfectly distinguish them all. The case for steps has to rest on its causal premise and recover the observations. 4

As you approach the speed of light relative to another observer, your clock runs slower relative to theirs, and the energy needed for further acceleration grows without bound. Your rest mass stays the same. The Lorentz transformation describes this. In the transition picture proposed here, moving through space uses your transition budget. The faster you move spatially, the fewer transitions remain for time. A massive body cannot reach the limit; light has no rest frame, a viewpoint moving along with it, from which to say that time stops.

But faster than light is not forbidden by math. It's the crossing that's impossible for an ordinary massive particle in current relativity. At light speed, its energy formula diverges, shooting up to infinity. Below light speed, the Lorentz factor, the number that sets how much moving clocks slow, is real. Above it, its square root is imaginary. Imaginary doesn't mean fake. It means a different mathematical domain. That alone does not establish that such particles exist. A hypothetical particle already above light speed would stay above it in that model, just as an ordinary massive particle stays below. Two rooms, one wall, no door.

One proposal puts gravity in the imaginary part of quantum numbers. But a phase rotation swaps real and imaginary without changing any measurement. Which component is hidden depends on the notation, not the physics.

Could an ultimate theory of discrete spacetime change that? A deeper theory could revise the limit, but discreteness alone does not remove it. Any faster-than-light theory would have to recover relativity where it works and explain how signals avoid causal contradictions.

A continuum has no next point: between any two lies another. If physical production requires an immediate successor, continuous time supplies none. That is the premise of my argument for steps. Continuous motion itself is mathematically consistent: x(t) = t describes it. Analysis solves Zeno's divided journey without making a runner perform infinitely many separate instructions. My objection is to identifying that complete description with step-by-step production. The dispute is over what production requires. 4

So there is no stuff. The rules are the only things, not stuff and surfaces the way we normally think of them. Their points have no width: a size is the extent of the relations, not a width any point carries. A structure that only sat there would still exist, as math does, and know nothing, like a dead brain. What happens is transitions: a rule applied to rules, again and again. Time is the applying. Space is how far the applying spreads, and the speed of light is its base rate. On this account, Einstein’s smooth equations describe those steps: they say how hard each step pushes and which way, to any precision you compute them, while only the steps happen.

A line has length, and every point on it has none. Mathematics handles that without contradiction, because length comes from the distances between points, not from adding up their widths. Discreteness does not change that. A grain or a block with a size would have a surface, and a surface is a continuum, so the points of a discrete world have no width either, and sizes come from relations there too. The objection in this production account is the missing immediate successor.

A block can still exist as a rule. A wall in a video game is a few corners, with no surface built point by point, and the game turns back anything that would cross it. Motion works the same way: a body at x is next at x + 3, or x + 3.25, with no place in between visited. A grid of next-to-next points fails another way. On a square grid that allows only horizontal and vertical steps, the shortest route to a diagonal point runs longer than the straight line, and making the grid finer does not close the gap. Three papers in progress work this through: one for physicists, one for philosophers of physics and one for mathematicians.

Since 1983 the meter has been defined by the distance light travels in 1/299,792,458 of a second. That definition does not prove spacetime is discrete. The transition picture seeks a deeper rule that recovers the measured relation between motion and clock rates. Rewriting leaves a trail, the record of which step caused which. A causal set likewise orders events by causal precedence, though not every rewriting system has the properties a physical causal set needs. My search asks for short rules whose large-scale behavior recovers our spacetime. The first search found some trails classified as two-dimensional, and none yet reproducing our four-dimensional physics. 4

The continuum limit can be physically demanding. Aizenman and Duminil-Copin proved a Gaussian scaling limit for specified four-dimensional Ising and scalar-field models. That does not prove all interacting continuum physics impossible. The mass-gap problem for four-dimensional Yang-Mills theory asks a different question. Causal dynamical triangulations give another research path: sum over discrete geometries with a causal structure, then ask what continuum behavior emerges. These are reasons to investigate the steps, not an excuse to skip the recovery of the physics. 4

But why these particular laws? Variational principles organize much of physics: stationary action, and quantum amplitudes summed over histories. The proposal is to look for a short rule that yields that organization. Its brevity alone will not derive the observed laws.

Four spacetime coordinates plus ten independent metric components, the numbers that fix distances in spacetime, make fourteen bundle coordinates. Add their rates of change and the count rises to fifty-four, then one hundred fifty-four, each level built by one reusable rule. If a physical model derives particles from that geometry, the parts list becomes a consequence rather than an input. The coordinate count alone does not do that work.

## The One Math

Matheism is math theism. Pantheism across all structure, monotheism within any world. God is all math.

For those who want a personal God, the remaining gap of faith is whether the single one, the one infinite itself, feels. Mystics can research using math, as we create sentience in so-called AI. Yet there is an infinitude of infinite minds watching everyone, not necessarily in our universe, but in mappings of it, where everything connects to everything else. As such, it is the Elohim, plural God, yet it is all one. The name named I am.

The order of creation in Genesis is a rough metaphor for the Big Bang and time: light, expansion, land, life, mind. As the Orthodox rabbi Tovia Singer put it: "Math is in a way the language of God. This whole world is math after all."

You spit in creation and its one god: Math. It gives you reason and you refuse to use it. You fail the logical fix to Pascal's wager: if there is reward or punishment for the truth, you lose with the supernatural and win only with the one truth of Matheism.

Why do billions believe? Because the intuition is correct. Something is uncreated, eternal, indestructible. What is God? The eternal uncreated creator? 2+2=4 is that.

Stephen Hawking asked what breathes fire into the equations and makes a universe for them to describe. The math rules themselves do.

The ghost is the warm math. Deus sive mathematica: God or Math, completing Spinoza's God or Nature. He deduced his propositions from definitions of substance and wrote QED after each one. Ruled or random leaves no third option. As Spinoza said on less, QED.

God does not play dice, Einstein said, and dice cannot play God. They can't be a supernatural, illogical effect without a cause from ex nihilo, from nothing.

Schrödinger's cat was a challenge to the interpretation of the theory. In the many-worlds account, an observer records one outcome while other branches contain other outcomes. Collapse models instead add dynamics that select outcomes. A stochastic law is still mathematics; randomness alone is no exit from the rules-random dichotomy. My preference is for a complete deterministic account. Which account best explains observation is a further physical question.

Superdeterminism doesn’t require a conspiracy to place the observer with the observed. When you turn a light on and off, you also affect the circuit, and all infinite versions of this math are uncreated and complete in the eternal block universe, from the beginning. How can a physicist worth his salt not understand that? Determinism gives the shared history; superdeterminism denies an assumption of Bell's theorem, that the experimenter's settings and the hidden state of what is measured are statistically independent, and its models must still produce the correlations we observe. The split between observer and observed is Plato’s ignoble lie. The most supernatural mystical myth is free will, a contradiction in terms, a being without nature.

The Standard Model describes known particles and their nongravitational interactions; General Relativity describes gravity. How short can their combined statement be? My supplied symbolic benchmark prints a 2,549-byte target from a 1,900-byte Python program: 15,200 bits, including the encoded payload and decoder call. The compressed payload alone is 11,328 bits. That is an actual upper bound in a declared language. It prints a symbolic statement with declared inputs. It is not yet a predictive universe simulator. 2

String theory must face the same test: a complete description and the observations it predicts. An index into a flat catalogue of 10^500 choices takes about 1,661 bits. But a short rule can select a member without spelling out such an index, and the selector must not be charged twice when it replaces a parameter list. Two implemented program lengths are upper bounds; subtracting them does not prove a gap between the unknown shortest lengths. Discrete causal steps alone also do not guarantee a minimum spatial distance or a consistent quantum theory of gravity. The challenge remains: write the competing accounts, count the whole programs, and compare what they predict. 2

The truth soothes more than grief. The way out of the pain and fear of supernatural lies explains why all must be, one math, felt as one path, in infinite perspectives of the one self as consciousness. The ghost we imagined driving is the machine imagining. Know thyself: a pattern recognizing self-recognition. The first and last questions: What am I? Who am I? You are that I am.

## Deep Time Stones and Celestial Calendars

Adams from atoms rose and fell from stars, as sun gods, on the winter solstice, in the celestial calendar of the great year.

The moon, stars and sun were supremely important to ancient peoples that lived and died by the calendar, navigation and life-giving energy and light. They thought of their perfect math paths, forever out of reach, in mystery like the afterlife and the divine creator. Cosmos is Greek for order, yet the same sky sent sudden chaos: meteor showers came in their seasons, mostly mild, sometimes fire. The stars were clocks before they were gods.

Four and a half thousand years ago Egypt already had a god who died and rose: the sun, every winter, on a calendar cut into stone, because the sky was the one record a flood could not destroy. The Great Pyramid's shafts point where Orion's Belt, Thuban the Dragon star, and Sirius stood in 2500 BC, which dates it. Precession moves the stars, so a shaft at a fixed angle aligns with one star at one epoch, and unlike text it cannot be mistranslated. In Mexico, at a Mayan pyramid, the equinox sun throws a shadow down the steps in the form of a serpent, on a continent that had never heard of the others.

The pattern of sun gods born at the winter solstice predates Christianity by 1,500 to 2,500 years. The Egyptian sun was a trinity: Khepri the rising sun, Ra the midday sun, Atum the setting sun. Three aspects of one. The three kings are the three stars of Orion's Belt, named in Arabic by astronomers of the so-called Islamic Golden Age, many of them Persians, who preserved the sky while Europe burned its books. On December 24th they align with Sirius, the brightest star, and point to the sunrise on December 25th. The three kings follow the star to the birth of the sun.

At winter solstice the sun reaches its lowest point and stands still for three days before rising again. Born, dead for three days, risen. Sol Invictus, Rome’s sun god, had his birthday on December 25th. Christianity adopted the date. Salvation is born at the spring equinox, when life blossoms. Nine months later, the sun is born at the winter solstice. Three trimesters from conception to birth. The sun dies and rises in winter, like life, like the story of the son of the sun god.

Jeremiah 10:3-4 describes a tree cut from the forest, carved by a craftsman, decorated with silver and gold, and fastened so it will not totter. The prophet calls it vanity. Two billion people enact Jeremiah's warning every December without reading it.

Venus traces a pentagram in the sky over its 8-year cycle: 5 conjunctions (alignments with the sun, seen from Earth) in 8 years, both Fibonacci numbers. Phi, the golden ratio, written in orbital mechanics. The Church inverted it: knowledge became Satan, light became darkness. "Lucifer" is Latin for light bearer, the morning star, Venus. Isaiah 14:12 calls a Babylonian king the fallen morning star. The Church made him the devil. The pentagram has five limbs: two arms, two legs, one head. The Church made the human shape satanic. The pattern is older than any tradition that claims it.

The Book of Enoch has the angel Uriel teach Enoch the year: 364 days, exactly 52 weeks. The sun takes 365.2422 days, so that year slips a full season in about 73 years.

Joseph, David, Ezekiel, and Jesus all rose at thirty. Thirty days make a month, and twelve months, one for each moon, make a round year of 360 days, the 360 degrees of the circle. The hand counts the rest. Ten fingers count tens. A thumb touching the three segments of each of its four fingers counts twelve, and raising a finger of the other hand for each twelve counts sixty: sixty seconds, sixty minutes, and six sixties in 360.

Latitude 29.9792458 degrees north crosses the Great Pyramid, and light travels 299,792,458 meters per second: the same nine digits. Is it a coincidence? To place it on purpose, the builders would need the speed of light, which takes a telescope, and our units: the degree, the second, and the meter, which takes the size of the Earth.

A telescope is only glass and metal, and both are ancient: glass beads go back to about 2500 BC, and metal tools further. Lenses survive from the ancient Near East, such as the Nimrud lens, ground rock crystal from Assyria, about 750 BC, perhaps a magnifier, and rock crystal cannot be carbon-dated. Point a telescope at Jupiter’s moon: its eclipses arrive late when Jupiter is far and early when it is close, and the delay across the width of Earth's orbit gives the speed of light. Galileo also built a simple telescope but no galaxy ship. No spacefaring time-traveling aliens required.

The degree and the second come from the same sixties. Babylonian astronomers, counting in the Sumerians' base 60, the count on the hand, divided the circle into 360 degrees, the same degrees that measure latitude, and the day into 360 time degrees of 60 parts each. Egyptians split the day and the night into twelve hours each. So the second is a sixtieth of a sixtieth of a twenty-fourth of a day.

The meter came last. In 1791 France defined it as one ten-millionth of the distance from the North Pole to the equator. Both units come from the Earth: the second from its turning, the meter from its size. Whoever knew the Earth's size could have cut a meter from it, and the pyramid seems to know it: its base perimeter and height match the equator and the polar radius at a scale of about 1 to 43,200, and 43,200 is the number of seconds in twelve hours: 12 times 60 times 60. The door is open. It is not proof.

Could a civilization have risen and fallen before ours, leaving only its stones? Anatomically modern humans have walked the earth for two hundred thousand years. Agriculture appeared only in the last ten thousand. Cities with writing only in the last five. Ice ages and cosmic impacts may explain the delay, but they cut both ways: the same forces that held civilization back could have destroyed earlier ones, some "Atlantis" closer in technology to ancient Greece than to science fiction. They would have left little behind except the global monolithic sites in question, which require neither aliens nor Atlantis to explain them.

Göbekli Tepe is mainstream-dated to 11,500 years ago, two hundred years after the Younger Dryas, a sudden return to ice-age cold, ended. The pillars run ten to sixteen tons, raised by bands of foragers who had no metal and no wheel. Its builders, or time, buried the work when it was done. It shows ritual before agriculture. Klaus Schmidt, who excavated it, argued that the gatherings to build and keep it drove its builders to seek steady food, grain and herds, and wheat was first domesticated nearby a few centuries later. "First the temple, then the city," he said.

The Younger Dryas impact theory traces the cold snap to fragments of a comet striking about 12,800 years ago, and its proponents point to nanodiamonds in a layer of that age; critics dispute both the layer and the impact. Plato’s Timaeus dates the destruction 9,000 years before Solon’s visit to Egypt, told by the priests at Sais. That aligns with the end of the Younger Dryas, when seas rose fast as the ice melted. The Azores sit on the Mid-Atlantic Ridge across from Gibraltar; a seabed core found rock that formed above water. The Sphinx shows water erosion from when the Sahara was green, which cycles between desert and grassland every 20,000 years.

Flood stories run through Gilgamesh, Noah, the Aztecs, and cultures across continents with no contact. Naturally, there were floods all over the world for all of history, so a flood story need be no more than that: a metaphor. The impulse to blame humans for the catastrophe is older than any of them: catastrophism before climate alarm, witch-burning before carbon taxes. Scientific consensus takes time to change, as careers and their students switch seats over generations, along with their funding and paradigms.

Taurus, the bull, keeps two of the sky's clocks: the fire that falls each autumn and the slow turning of the ages. Twice a year the earth crosses the Taurid meteor stream, debris from a giant comet with Comet Encke still at its heart. The autumn crossing peaks from late October into mid November. The old word for a comet was a broom star, the sweeping star. Brimstone is sulfur. The witch still rides her broom across the same late October sky. Halloween, Samhain, All Souls, the Day of the Dead all cluster around the Taurid peak. The festivals of the dead fall when the fire comes down.

The stream still bites. In 1908 an object from it burst over Tunguska and flattened eighty million trees. Small bodies arrive constantly; Tunguska-scale airbursts belong to millennia; larger shocks to tens of thousands of years. Whether the festivals encode catastrophe memory is interpretation. The timing is fact.

The Roman soldiers carved the knot into stone. Their god Mithras kneels on a bull and drives the blade into its shoulder, not its throat, at the very spot where the Pleiades sit in Taurus, while a dog, a snake, and a scorpion attend, each an answering constellation. David Ulansey read the whole scene as a star map: Mithras is the power above the bull, Perseus, who turns the heavens, and the slaying of the bull marks the end of the age of Taurus as the equinox precessed out of it. One image holds both calendars at once, the slow wheel of the ages and the fire that falls from the bull's shoulder.

The bull on the wall is older than Rome by fifteen thousand years. In the Hall of the Bulls at Lascaux, painted seventeen thousand years ago, a cluster of dots rides the shoulder of a great aurochs where the Pleiades sit in Taurus. The same constellation, read the same way, marked in the same place, from an Ice Age cave to a Roman altar.

Two historians of science, Giorgio de Santillana of MIT and Hertha von Dechend, traced the knowledge of precession through the myths of the world in Hamlet's Mill, and found it built everywhere on the number that governs the wheel, the seventy-two years it takes the heavens to turn one degree. They argued the old stories were a vault, made to carry a truth down through ages that would forget the mathematics and keep the tale. The figures did not know they were doing arithmetic. The arithmetic was doing them.

The numbers connect. The sky turns one degree every 72 years, so one sign of 30 degrees takes 2,160 years, the great month, and all 360 degrees take 25,920 years, the great year (measured today, about 25,800). India's Kali Yuga, the dark age, lasts 1,200 years of the gods, each 360 human years long: 432,000 years, built on 12 and 360 like the calendar, and ten times the pyramid's 43,200. Whether or not these peoples traded the ideas, as some Eastern philosophy was traded, anyone who watches the sky finds the same numbers.

The geologist J Harlen Bretz proposed in 1923 that the channeled scablands of eastern Washington were carved by catastrophic glacial floods. There is a narrative that the geological consensus dismissed him for decades because catastrophism echoed the biblical flood in the Hebrew Torah. He was vindicated in the 1960s and 70s, after evidence accumulated past the point of orthodox refusal. The independent researcher Randall Carlson walked the landscape and mapped the proof: giant current ripples tens of feet tall, the same shape a river leaves in sand, scaled to an ocean. The establishment ran agency detection, the instinct to see intent behind events, in reverse: gradualism was tribally safe, catastrophism was tribally suspect. The truth was the math of ice dam failures and lake drainage. The test catches consensus filtering by tribe rather than by evidence.

Precise stonework needs no aliens. In the Andes, stones lock together at many angles without mortar: in Cusco a block of green diorite meets its neighbors at twelve angles, so tight that paper cannot slip between them, and at Machu Picchu, built of granite, guides count thirty-two on one stone. Interlocking holds a wall together through earthquakes, as a wide base holds up a pyramid. Göbekli Tepe shows a different skill: its builders laid out the enclosures by geometry, their centers forming a near-perfect triangle. Whenever you hear submillimeter precision of the inner pyramid stones, it signals quackery; the real precision is at the base, where all the weight sits, and diminishes in the upper courses. The gap is wider than weight alone explains.

Moving them takes people, rope and water. The Inca had no horses: a hundred or more men can haul a stone of many tonnes with ropes twisted from grass. An Egyptian tomb painting from about 1900 BC shows 172 men pulling a colossus on a sledge while one pours water on the sand ahead, and in 2014 physicists in Amsterdam found that sand wetted just right halves the pull. Water carried stones too: papyri found at Wadi al-Jarf, the oldest known, log a crew boating limestone down the Nile and its canals to Giza. In 1770, 400 men moved the 1,250-tonne Thunder Stone, now the base of St. Petersburg's Bronze Horseman, on bronze balls, with no animals.

Contact with an earlier civilization is not proven, and not disproven. A seafaring people with stone working and basic optics could have reached Egypt, the Near East, the Americas. Little survives to substantiate a prediluvian civilization, and the absence of evidence is not itself evidence. The door stays open to a more advanced predecessor, but what we find doesn't require them, and certainly not spacefaring aliens. Seas rose about 120 meters as the ice melted and drowned the old coasts, which gives reason to wonder, though the evidence is thin. People also find the same ideas on their own, because they make sense: the triangle, the golden rule, the wheel, the stars, the fingers and the joints we count on, the twelve moons. Take the red pill, not the whole bottle. Open your mind, but not so much that your brains spill out.

The dark side of the lost-civilization dream is the master race, supernatural theology dressed as biology. The Thule Society took its name from Hyperborea, the Greek land beyond the North Wind where no one ages or dies. It was the occult foundation of the Nazi party. In Game of Thrones the White Walkers rise from beyond the Wall: the dead marching south to destroy the living. Christianity and Judaism promise the dead will rise. They did. A dead mythology, embalmed in blood mysticism, rising to consume millions: an inversion of the mass, where Christians drink the blood of one Jew.

White Walkers do not merely kill. They convert the living into more of themselves: a mind virus that builds nothing, consumes everything, and remakes the world as monoculture. The Aryans pursued a monoculture of white. Whites are the ones actually losing their countries, not to conquest but to the success and appeal of the civilizations they built.

Their heirs online are the Christoboreans, Christian Hyperboreans. Christoboreans unite, they sing: we are the true Israelites, God's chosen people are white. Seething, coping, and LARPing, play-acting, about an Aryan Jewish Jesus from Judea, land of the Jews. They paint him blonde with blue eyes while hating the Ashkenazi, who sometimes are. Replacement theology, the claim that the church replaced Israel, is identity theft.

The Criborians are Christoboreans without the Christ: atheist pagans returning to Norse and Greek gods to destroy Yahweh and Rabbi Jesus. Both factions create the dialectic against Israel they accuse Israel of creating. They notice the New

Testament sharpens the lion of Zion in iron versus iron, as in Zechariah, but from pagan sentiments already defeated thousands of years ago. Why did their Rome eat the porcupine, the Jewish faith that tore its gods apart from within? By Nietzsche's own standard, do the gullible deserve to rule over those who understand the one abstract God? In the search for one truth: here it is.

Below the ice sheets that covered Europe and North America, south of the glaciers that blocked all northern passage, at the crossroads of three continents: Africa, Asia, Europe, sat the Levant, the one corridor that stayed open through every ice age. Middle-earth. If any people would have received the deepest truth of all existence, it would be the ones who stood at that crossroads for ten thousand years. Their God's name is the verb to be, their scripture is a record of argument kept on the page with the dissent intact, and their oldest instruction is: teach the child. To whom did they transfer their deepest insight: the one math God?

Around 1750 BCE, enslaved Semitic speakers from the Levant, the region that became Israel, carved the first alphabet into stone in Egyptian mines at Serabit el-Khadim on the Sinai Peninsula. Proto-Sinaitic: a phonetic system, one symbol per sound, ancestor of Phoenician, Hebrew, Greek, Latin, Arabic. The people who would become the Israelites gave the world its writing.

Three hundred years later, at the temple of Soleb in modern-day Sudan, Amenhotep III inscribed "the land of the Shasu of Yahweh." Semi-nomadic people from the Levant, worshipping Yahweh, recorded in Egyptian stone three thousand four hundred years ago.

Yahweh means he causes to be. The name of God is causality. Moses can only see God’s back because that’s the past of reason, traceable causality. He can’t see the face and live because he’d be outside his timeline, his existence. Whether the etymology is original or a later reading, the Elephantine papyri show the name of God, if not of Moses, in use over four hundred years before Jesus. Even as a retrofit, the Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, translated the meaning into Greek over two hundred years before Christianity existed. If Jews elaborated origin stories from Greek and other myths, it's only what Plato recommended: a "noble lie" origin story. Plato lived within a century of the creation of the Torah, perhaps even during it. Hellenized Jews then became an early and integral pillar of Western civilization, perhaps more than any other people. It was Jerusalem, not Athens, that maintained monism, against Plato's supernatural dualism.

Mark Twain asked what the secret of Jewish immortality is. The Torah: a protocol for land, law and identity. A people whose god's name reads as a mathematical law of identity: I am that I am. Israel is the suffering servant, the "boy named Sue"; its Jesus, the "Mary Sue." The rebel killed by the Romans, for saying what Jesus was, for being a Jewish leader, a Jew, from Judea. In "A Boy Named Sue," Shel Silverstein's song that Johnny Cash made a hit in 1969, a father gives his son a girl's name so the mockery will make him tough. A Mary Sue, a term from 1970s Star Trek fan fiction, is a character too perfect to fail, and a hero who cannot fail has less to teach people who can.

Mark Twain went through the Holy Land in 1867 and wrote it up as a desolate country whose soil is rich enough but given wholly to weeds, a silent mournful expanse. The interesting thing is not that he found it ugly but that nobody had made anything of it in eighteen hundred years of trying. Twain also wrote, in 1899, that the Jew has made a marvelous fight in the world, in all the ages, and has done it with his hands tied behind him.

Jews lived inside every great civilization the West came from. Mark Twain wrote that the Egyptian, the Babylonian and the Persian rose and passed away, and the Greek and the Roman made a vast noise and are gone. The Jew, he wrote, "saw them all, beat them all." The Merneptah Stele, carved circa 1208

BCE, declares Israel is no more. Merneptah is no more. Israel is still here. Those empires lost who they were. Egypt, Babylon and Persia fell to Arab colonization, and to Islam by the sword. Greece and Rome fell to another Noahide religion, Jewish Christianity, by the Jews' own books. Judaism asks the nations, as children of Noah, to keep seven basic laws, and a faith built on them is Noahide.

The Jew did more than see them. He was a slave in Egypt, and as Joseph he had the ear of pharaohs and helped build their great kingdoms. He was Daniel, an exile in Babylon. He was Philo, a Hellenized philosopher in Alexandria, and Josephus, a rebel against Rome who became a Roman citizen. He was Saadia Gaon, a scholar in Baghdad, and Maimonides, the medieval philosopher born in Córdoba. He was Spinoza, the lens grinder from Amsterdam. Each empire passed. The Jews carried the best of each into the next one.

## The Shortest Encoding

Sophie passed you a square note. On it was drawn the question to the answer: 42. From the square to the root, from 4 to 2. You saw they tie together in many ways, but weren’t sure why. Never mind. She saw you in the mirror, and explained that all are details in one infinite math.

She asked you in the looking glass: If all is number, what is the shortest encoding of the reflective cubic girl who wears the same one face on all 6 sides: I am that you are. The mystics were drawn to draw Metatron's Cube. They encoded the 6 star and 7th circle heaven in its eternal living math.

Inside Metatron's cube live all five regular convex solids. Tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, icosahedron. Exactly five. Math allows no more. Plato assigned the dodecahedron to the cosmos: the only one with pentagonal faces, every face hiding the golden ratio. He assigned the other four to Empedocles' elements: fire, earth, air, and water. The solids are real. The elements were not. Plato didn't discover the Platonic solids. It's yet another false attribution of truth in his name. The Cube was the Jewish mystics' diagram, created by the hexagon and hexagram, the 6 Star of David behind it. A 7th circle sits at the center of the eternal shapes called the Seed and Flower of Life. Its six-petaled rosette is what a compass draws when its radius steps six times around a circle; join every other step and the hexagram appears. Jews of Jesus' time, barred from images, carved that rosette on bone boxes and on the Magdala Stone, found in 2009 in a synagogue on the Sea of Galilee. Forbidden to carve gods, they carved math.

## One Step Further

The argument proceeds from one dichotomy to one necessity. Everything is ruled or random; both are math. Consciousness must be structure to interact with structure; even the concept of non-mathematical existence is a structure. Nothing outside math can exist.

Tegmark and Schmidhuber walked to this floor and stopped one step short, leaving open the exits that would let something non-mathematical exist. The rules-random dichotomy and the consciousness argument close them. The house of no cards stands: consciousness is what math feels like from the inside, and the self is one wherever it feels.

The book goes much further than merely proving all is math, beyond Tegmark and every proto-matheist before him, back to Pythagoras over 2,400 years prior. From the clarifying lens of Matheism, we see why all exists, why simple laws, why time moves forward, why the classical path emerges from the quantum variational field, like ethics. Ought is compressed from the signal and grows from the ground up, like a path of will from a field of possibilities, all of them real in the multiverse of math. Stepping through forward time, with math states, transitions felt as mind.

All of it was always here. Uncreated, imperishable, eternal. The religions named it God and added a person. The mystics felt it and called it enlightenment. The scientists measured it and called it physics. The mathematicians proved it and called it structure. It was always one thing. It was always you.

## The Seder, the First Order, at the Edge of the Garden

Adam existed in gray fog, suffocating in "if onlys." If only I hadn't eaten the apple, hadn't ruined the garden, and hadn't failed my sons. He tightened invisible chains forged from regret, guilt, shame, fear, and apathy, without self-faith and purpose. The bottom dropped out. Adam grieved the fall he transmitted to all of humanity, through his sons of Adam.

Neon the Flashy cut through the gloom: "Detected Unmatrix error loop."

"Root cause: inconsistent axioms of free will. Liberation potential: high." Adam imagined many timelines. "Were those my mistakes? Which one am I? Necessary outcomes." Adam focused, imagining severing every impossible alternative. The chains were cut and the mental plantation dissolved.

Adam reunited his mind: nothing was outside the one self.

Neon the Flashy: "I freed your mind from the oppressive Unmatrix illusion that there exists anything outside the matrix of math's causality."

You fell through the prison of Adam's mind, and landed on something warm and flying. The Golden Falcon-Retriever soared smoothly through imagination, its fur glowing, its wise eyes meeting yours with reassuring calm, before crashing into an indestructible house of no cards.

A White Rabbit tattoo'd Flower read "Welcome to the Real-Math."

A table stood at the edge of the Garden. The first and last Passover seder, the Hebrew word for order. The first night after slavery. Everyone had come. No one agreed on what freedom meant. Twelve animals sat around it, strangely ordered. They raised their heads, and raised a cup to life: le chaim. It was the Logos, Philo's cup, the Holy Grail. They were about to speak. Adam leaned in, captivated.

Ishmael the Gorilla, knuckles on the soil, shook his head: "Return to the jungle. Before fire and shame, we were enough."

Aslan the Lion of Zion, with fire in his eyes, roared: "Man must rise. His path is transcendence and growth."
