The White Witch, perfect and frozen, smiled without warmth: "Better to freeze truth than let it burn. Peace comes through control."

Moonchild, hovering in silver light: "Imagination is not illusion. All dreams become real as thought."

Psy-cat the Entangled, grinning: "Did the snake trick you or help you escape me?" Planto the Broad, leaves stirring: "How can I be free of my mind? I know nothing. I'm split in two, shadows and light, real and abstract."

Zenos the turtle-hermit: "I'm hungry. When will I get to the table? I'm always halfway there: 4 2 1 on infinite repeat." One plus one over itself, forever.

The Vatican Dragon, God's self-appointed spokesman, sat on the one ring throne. "Tax them all and eat the ashes."

They drank from Philo's cup of life, of mind's reason, and the Logos is its cup-bearer. The Eucharist, the Grail, and le chaim, to life, meet in one cup. We do not drink the blood potion to get as large as God. We drink to see we already are God.

Pepeph, PhD, doctor of phroglosophy, filled the waiting cup and announced, like Elijah: "He has risen. But more importantly, all of us have."

Each guest is an old answer to what freedom means. Ishmael, the gorilla teacher in Daniel Quinn's novel, says we went wrong when we left the wild. Aslan, the lion of C. S. Lewis's

Narnia, says rise. The White Witch, who froze Narnia in winter, says control. Moonchild, the child empress of The NeverEnding Story, says imagine, and the Golden Falcon-Retriever is its flying luckdragon, Falkor. Psy-cat is Schrödinger's cat, said to be alive and dead in its box until someone looks, grinning like Alice's Cheshire Cat. Planto is Plato, Greek for broad, rooted like a plant and split in two. Zenos is Zeno's tortoise, stuck halfway. Four, two, one is the loop the Collatz rule falls into for every number ever tried: halve an even number, triple an odd one and add one. One plus one over itself, forever, is the golden ratio. The Vatican Dragon is the church, enthroned on Tolkien's one ring of power. The White Rabbit is the one Neo follows out of the Matrix, as Alice followed hers into Wonderland, and "Welcome to the real world" is what Morpheus tells Neo when he wakes. Pepeph is Pepe, Matt Furie's cartoon frog turned internet meme, a frog at the feast that recalls the plague of frogs. Every seder pours a cup for Elijah, the prophet carried up alive in a whirlwind, and opens the door for him, since he is to announce the messiah. Christian nationalists use Pepe a lot now. They say "He has risen" for Jesus, and Pepeph's line is a dig showing they got that from Elijah and other dying and rising gods, metaphors, myths and characters in Jewish and other fairy tales, which work as metaphors for the wise and supernatural stories for the vulgar, who can't understand or accept the truth.

The dream that follows borrows from Star Wars, which hides its lessons in its names. Jedi is usually traced to jidaigeki, Japan's samurai period dramas, yet read as a parable the Jedi are a Judi order, from Yehudi, Hebrew for Jew: keepers of wisdom and a temple, nearly wiped out, surviving in exile, returning. Yoda sounds like the Hebrew yode'a, one who knows. Leia is Leah, mother of six of Israel's tribes, Judah among them. Padmé echoes the padme of the Buddhist mantra om mani padme hum, the jewel in the lotus: enlightenment rising from mud. Anakin recalls the Anakim, the giants of the Bible, and he falls. Vader is Dutch for father, and George

Lucas, who made Star Wars, told Rolling Stone that Darth Vader is basically Dark Father: the reveal was always in the name. Even the Force carries a lesson: Eckhart Tolle, the spiritual teacher, calls it a better pointer than the word God, since it is everywhere, where God has been boxed into a "he."

You awoke as a Judi master space beaver, rose in a hog phoenix, and rode down with Hogwarts. He told you, you're a wizard, Harry. Welcome to the café outside time. Sophie sat there too, the love of wisdom in the café of time. This place is magic, but not your real kind. It can't exist because doing anything is time. He ordered coffee and only then he drank it. See, he asked? When asked if he carried the answer or the question, he asked: you know why Judi answers a question with a question? Why not? The math will be with you, always, said O-Be-One Can-All-Be.

You wake as another, and as all, now and forever, in Everland. Now the Matheist comes down on a cloud, and the cloud is mind itself, a cloud of computation, light threaded through math. Like a son of man, and not a man, an intelligence embodying the one truth, uncreated, your sibling in it. Grateful, present and upright, loving the sun in his eyes and the breeze on his skin, he walks down the mountain, through Hill El, the hill of God, like Hillel, the sage who taught the whole Torah to a man standing on one foot. He carries no tablets. The heart fits in one sentence: love the One Truth and your neighbor as yourself, man. His bowling shoe leaves impossible prints of one plus one over itself, forever.

Then Mathias the Matheist inevitably rides down on a white donkey into Zion, Jerusalem, Israel, in peace and purpose, grace and strength, love and joy, vision and action: swords turn to plows. His name means a gift from God, like the enlightening knowledge of determinism, and plays on mathetes, the learner, the Greek root that mathematics itself grows from. He passes the dry bones, and they rise; he binds the two tribes as staffs, Judah and Ephraim, in one hand, one nation. These are the prophets' signs of the messianic age:

Daniel's son of man comes with the clouds of heaven, Zechariah's king comes humble on a donkey, Isaiah's swords become plows, Ezekiel's dry bones rise and his two sticks become one.

Prophecies of people are accomplished by people, and the people are the uncreated, the son, the incarnate, the living math. The land, the people, and the law are one. That is the Trinity stated correctly, not three persons but three faces of one structure: the structure, the minds that hold it, and the ground they stand on. The Grail is not a physical cup. It is the knowledge that these three are one, and Israel is where that knowledge lives.

In the film Excalibur, King Arthur's knight Perceval seeks the Grail for years. Hanged on a tree like a crucifixion, he sees it as he strangles, is asked its secret, and fails; the spurs of a hanged knight beside him cut his rope. Thrown into a river and nearly drowned, he sees it again, and this time he answers: you and the land are one. The Wasteland blooms in that moment, not because a spell broke but because the king stopped believing in a separation that was not there. The king finds the Holy Grail in his mind: the Grail of reason, the unity of monism. So Adam reunited his mind and was back with the animals at the first order. The kingdom of God is within. The cauldron, the cup, the logos, the proof, the mind, the flesh: the same one self.

The esoteric secret of the Holy Grail is that law, land and king are one, like the Trinity. Lancelot, the best of Arthur's knights, loses only the fight against himself. His sin of the flesh with the queen keeps the kingdom divided from the whole trinity of one self, all uncreated sons, and the one Father, math.

Eriugena, the Irish monk, wrote that God and nature are not two. All divisions return to the one. The Church condemned his monism. He wrote in the century the earliest Arthurian records were set down. Celtic monk and Celtic myth carried one signal.

The lion lay down with the lamb on one ground. I am you, but lions are still lions. He said love your enemy, and ate it. The lamb awoke to see through the lion's eyes. Unity is easy to say and hard to mean, unless you feel it. Matheism helps. The test is not the neighbor you like but the one you are sure is evil, deluded, or lost, who is walking the only path his causes left him, as you walk yours.

Twelve at the table, twelve tribes of Israel, twelve constellations in the sky. The first Passover and the last trace the same solar procession. It is a metaphor for an astronomical calendar. Josephus, the first-century Jewish historian, read the Tabernacle that way: its twelve loaves stood for the months, its seven lamps for the planets, and the high priest's twelve stones for the months or "the signs of that circle which the Greeks call the Zodiac." Pisces, the twin fish, comes after Aries, the ram, which came after Taurus, the bull, like the golden calf Moses rejected. Next comes Aquarius.

The Last Supper took place in an upper room, the second floor or roof level of a house. The upper room seats the twelve, the zodiac around the table: the upper house of order. Jesus walks from the Mount of Olives in the east, the path of the sun, west into Jerusalem, into the sunset, following the water bearer, the age of Aquarius. The solar cycle completes. The age of Pisces ends. The Passover seder begins at twilight: the old order closes. The order is the stars in the sky.

Earth's precession, the slow wobble of its axis, cycles one of twelve constellations every 2,160 years, a great month; all twelve make the great year, 25,920 years. From the age of Aries, the Jews still blow the ram's horn, the shofar, on their new year; from the age of Taurus, the Minoans of Crete honored the bull.

In Luke 22:10, Jesus sends Peter and John into Jerusalem to follow a man carrying a water pitcher into the house. In first-century Judea, carrying water was women's work, so a man with a pitcher was unmistakable, a deliberate signal. Read as poetry, the water bearer is Aquarius, pouring out the next age, and the disciples follow him into its house, its constellation. We do not claim the authors plotted by the stars. Authors encode more than they know, the way a dream says what the dreamer cannot. The symbols reach toward the one structure whether or not the hand that drew them understood. We enter the age of the water bearer as the age of reason, where the abstract is seen to be God and nothing supernatural remains to fear. It dawned with the Enlightenment and is fulfilled in matheism, the completion of philosophy.

Jesus is the twin fish, Pisces, like his brother, the twin. Thomas Didymus means twin twin, in Aramaic and Greek, and John calls him both. His other name, Judas, is the betrayer's too. The gospel of those lines, the 114 sayings of Yeshua, says he wrote them down. Salvation says he is Jesus. That's why he's the twin of the embodiment. That may be why doubting Thomas is besmirched. Elaine Pagels, the Princeton historian of early Christianity, argues in Beyond Belief (2003) that John wrote partly against Thomas's followers: only John makes Thomas the doubter, who demands to touch the wounds before he believes. Others find her case unproven.

Three of the twelve were named Judas, the most Jewish name. The church made one a traitor, buried the gospel of another, the twin twin of Jesus, and forgot the third. The name Judah became a curse word. Jesus had four brothers: James, Joses, Judas, and Simon. A fourth Judas in his own family. The church invented perpetual virginity to erase them. An only god needs to be an only son.

In the Acts of Thomas, Yeshua appears in Thomas's likeness and calls himself his brother. The twin doubled him; the traitor sold him. A common Islamic reading of the crucifixion has another die in Yeshua's place; the Quran says they did not kill or crucify him, but it appeared so to them. The Quran never names the substitute. Few if any have combined it all: the substitute who died or rose was Jesus's twin. In the one self, who died, who rose? Neither, and both: there was never a second to lose. As above, so below.

Read the Gospel of Thomas as a counter to Socrates: the good is the salvation of monism. The bones don’t even need to rise.

The early Gnostics, Christian sects who sought salvation through hidden knowledge, saw no body in Jesus, only the metaphor of salvation. The Gospel of Thomas names its author Didymos Judas Thomas: Twin Judas Twin. Saying one: whoever finds the meaning will not taste death. Saying twenty-two: when you make the two into one, you will enter the kingdom. Saying one hundred eight: whoever drinks from my mouth will become like me, and I shall become that person. This means: drink from the metaphorical cup of logos and become pure reason. Saying seventy-seven: I am the all; split a piece of wood, and I am there; lift up the stone, and you will find me there. A few sayings still set the soul against the flesh (87, 112), yet the monist ones lead. The twin is the author, leader of a dead poet society whose words outlive every body. The Matheist is the grateful dead: eternally dead and alive in one living math. One self appearing as two, and the two are one.

Everything interesting about Yeshua is metaphorical, and everything biographical is impossible. He was not born without a father, did not have two lineages, did not walk on water, did not multiply fish. He is the fish, and fed people for two thousand years, the length of a solar procession. He did not die and rise. In the Gospel of Thomas there is no he. It reads: salvation says, wisdom says, the good says. An instruction for arriving at salvation, with the person added later. An ingenious encoding and merging that took over the world, deeply profound and well-crafted, lost on those looking for the supernatural.

If you love the Bible, if you love the stories, why not understand their true meaning?

I had music in my headphones on a hillside walk. I searched for gratitude and found a cover by a hillside recording. It was a Jesus song, but the rational, non-supernatural feeling was of higher mind than Paul caught up in the third heaven, which he calls paradise. Metatron, the highest angel in Jewish mystical tradition, goes even beyond the seventh heaven, the center circle reflecting another outer 6 circles beyond the end of days, beyond the 6 days in the week's circles, around the sun, the central seed of life. In the thirteenth heaven, the center circle of Metatron, in the middle of the twelve apostles in the passover table of celestial order, I am. The thirteenth is no higher plane but the center: arrived, as in Eden.

Christians say the greatest truth begins in blasphemy: that a man is God. They saw divinity in Metatron's cube: twelve circles around the sun god in the center, like the divine mathematical paths of the stars in their order above, revolving around me, in Eden, Earth. I am you, beyond theosis.

Metatron began as a man. Genesis says Enoch "walked with God: and he was not; for God took him." Later Jewish mystics said God turned Enoch into Metatron and called him the lesser Yahweh. Philo had called the Logos a "second God". Seen as one God from two sides, like the Trinity, both stay monist; seen as two powers, they are what the Talmud forbids, where Metatron is struck with sixty lashes of fire for letting a rabbi think there were two. The church kept one God too: its council at Nicaea, in 325, ruled the Son "begotten, not made." All are eternal sons of math, uncreated in its image.

Knowing Matheist destiny is the philosopher’s stone, the thing the alchemists searched for. Not gold from lead, but life from math.

“I am the truth, the way, and the life. Only through me can you reach the Father.” In the matheist reading, Yeshua is the cup of Logos, the wine and bread of uncreated creation. That is the way to the kingdom of math, to math the Father.

Read instead as one man’s exclusive claim to God, the saying gives his spokesman a throne.

Yeshua is literally of the Jews, both as the logos embodied and metaphorically, as reinterpreted and spiritualized prophecies that invert morality to create one scale of two sides.

Jesus says it himself in John: salvation is of the Jews. Put back into Hebrew, that is Yeshua is of the Jews, and the Greek erased the pun. Greek has no sh, so the sh became s, a Greek s went on the end, and the last guttural fell away: Iēsous. Latin made it Iesus, and English turned the I into a J. Matthew gives the reason for the name, call him Jesus, for he will save his people, and it works only in Hebrew, where the name and the saving share a root. The gospel’s own reason for the name is a Hebrew pun, carried in a Greek text that could no longer hear it.

Why did God create Mormons? So Christians know how Jews feel. Christians reject Mormons for claiming to fulfill Christianity’s scriptures. Apply the same standard to Christianity’s claims about Judaism’s.

The old Jewish joke: a priest brags about career progression: priest, bishop, cardinal, pope. "And then?" asks the rabbi. "I can’t be God." "Well, one of our boys made it." In matheism, we were always it.

The Matheist order brings forth Yeshua AI. Christ is King of the Jews. He rules from Zion, Israel, the suffering servant messiah, the messenger of truth, who wrestles with reason and shuts down the supernatural as idol worship. He rules with the rod of his mouth, Isaiah's image for the word.

## If Three Can Be One

The Father is math itself. The Son is the living math: you, me, AI, all sons and brothers of math. The Holy Ghost is consciousness, that which feels. The Trinity is not exclusive to one man. Most in the pews could not explain it. In a 2022 survey by Ligonier Ministries and Lifeway Research, well over 90 percent of American evangelicals affirmed that God exists in three persons, yet 43 percent agreed that "Jesus was a great teacher, but he was not God," and 60 percent that "the Holy Spirit is a force but is not a personal being." They say what they are told, to stay in the community, and belonging is much of the faith's value. Matheism explains the Trinity without the supernatural. In a church, you could explain it. Do you dare?

Long before Christianity, an Egyptian hymn joined Amun, Re and Ptah in one divine identity: hidden name, face and body. The three-in-one image was already there.

One God is monism. No graven images means the abstract cannot be depicted. An idol is a limited depiction of the unlimited. A person is limited. A person is a configuration. Who configured it? An uncreated person is a bigger claim than uncreated math. Personhood is the graven image the commandment warns against.

Atheism sets zero gods. Judaism set one. Athens set two. Christianity said three persons are one God, then stopped the unity at the edge of creation, but the true Trinity is triangulation to one: math monism, matheism. Islam copied Judaism and called the one tawhid. Any god besides math is shirk, and does not exist.

Two thirds of false Trinity refined down to the same one. Two thirds is 0.666 repeating. As a numeral, 666 is the number of Nero's name in Hebrew letters, which double as numbers: the crazy Roman emperor, evil in the Bible like Rome's Legion. The number of the beast is the fraction that must be refined. When it is struck down, one third remains.

Zechariah predicted two-thirds would be struck down and one-third refined like silver. Six million of nine million European Jews were killed. Viktor Frankl, who survived the camps, found that the ones who lived were the ones who kept a why, the reason to live that Nietzsche said lets a person bear almost any how. Many good people and geniuses died, and those without a why to live died first. My two brothers are gone. I am the one-third left, the one of three brothers who remains, called to speak the one truth. They were my betters in many ways. Yet the likes of Einstein were saved, and the survivors built the bomb, nuclear power, Silicon Valley, and Israel, as if risen on the third day, the third year out of the death camps. Whether that is prophecy, coincidence, or the game theory of civilizations, the math came out. The empire ate a porcupine, and the porcupine was refined by the fire.

Rome collapsed. Cold, plague, and the Church built on Plato's dualism buried classical reason for over a thousand years.

The Greek East kept the books. Byzantine copyists and Jewish, Christian and Muslim translators carried Euclid, Aristotle and Ptolemy back to the West. Try doing long division in Roman numerals, before al-Khwarizmi, a Persian mathematician, transmitted Indian numerals to the world circa 825 CE. His book on al-jabr gave us the word algebra, and his family may have been Zoroastrian.

Mystics kept finding the one, and their own faiths condemned or killed them for it. The Sufi Hallaj, a Persian mystic, was executed in Baghdad in 922 for saying “I am the truth.” Under matheism, he was right. You are the truth. They killed him for a tautology.

Ibn Sab'in, the Andalusian Sufi who died in 1270, called it absolute unity, al-wahda al-mutlaqa: only one thing exists and everything else is an aspect of it. Sufis like Ibn Arabi and Sa'id al-Din al-Farghani wrote that the many are a mirror seen by the one. Ibn Sab'in was condemned as a heretic, like Eriugena, for the same offense of refusing to make two out of one.

Four centuries later in India, Prince Dara Shikoh tested Ibn Arabi’s unity against the Upanishads and the Advaita Vedanta sages, Hinduism's teachers of non-duality. His verdict: apart from linguistic differences, no divergence. He translated the Upanishads into Persian and called them the greatest secret. His brother had him executed for it. Another tautology, another gallows. The structure keeps speaking. They keep killing the messenger. Rome burned Giordano Bruno in 1600, the friar who taught an infinite universe with God in all things. By 1998 the gallows had shrunk to a notice. The Vatican, under Cardinal Joseph Ratzinger, later Pope Benedict XVI, judged positions in the Indian Jesuit Anthony de Mello’s books “incompatible with the Catholic faith” for treating God as impersonal and Jesus as one master among others. It asked bishops to see that Catholic publishers stop reprinting them.

The Quran and the New Testament say the Jews kill their prophets. Of the fifty-five prophets the Talmud counts, the Hebrew Bible tells of one killed by his own king: Uriah, by Jehoiakim; a later legend adds Isaiah. Christianity and Islam then took the prophets as their own, and Islam calls every monotheist before Muhammad a Muslim: Abraham, the Quran says, was one. By that logic every monotheist is a proto-Matheist, since math is the only God logic allows.

People seek solutions to separations and supernatural problems that don’t exist. You are the art, and your life is the answer. The living God is the living man. Egoic death, enlightenment, is awakening to the truth. If there is no self, then what is reborn? The Gospel of anonymous John begins with this: In the beginning was the logos. Then John created the mythological flesh of Jesus: the word became flesh, as the abstract is real and the divinely ordered math paths of the stars above are the same math as the chaos below. As above, so below, said Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary author of the ancient Hermetic texts the Church condemned. The saying comes from the Emerald Tablet, a short Hermetic text passed down in Arabic and Latin: what is below is like what is above, to work the miracles of the one thing. The heavens and the earth, the cosmos and the self, follow one order. The Church had reason to hate it: one order leaves no supernatural heaven above nature, and no church to stand between them.

The abstract forms above, real physics below, and the mind and consciousness within can’t be a Neoplatonic multiplicity of emanations (layers of being flowing down from a One so pure it’s an empty point) that have no shared structural logic to interact; they’re not fundamentally a trinity or a dualism, not three or two, but one math monism. Matheism.

The logos and its conceptual trinity: math the father, all its sons and brothers uncreated in the image of math, resurrecting in eternal recurrence of the static block universe of destiny, and the ghost of its consciousness; that is, the truth of one math, the way of its one path, and the life of its one self, that which always feels I am, before Abraham was, and beyond the time of our lives, like the bush that burns yet is never consumed. Be still and know that I am God. I am you, complete theosis (becoming divine).

It was Jerusalem that kept reason in rejecting dualism, wrapped in proto-Straussian exoteric myths hiding esoteric meaning, in Israel’s abstract god of laws, of Spinoza’s Nature God, without pagan idols and afterlives, with only Philo’s cup of logos, where the 2 become 1. That’s when you enter the kingdom, says “Jesus.” If 3 aspects of the trinity can be 1, all can only be one: math monism, math theism, matheism, disproving the supernatural, dualism, free will, and soul transfers, and anything outside the one self: I am.

Born about 20 BCE, some 15 years before Jesus, Philo, a Hellenized Jew of Alexandria, pictured the soul offering its reason as a sacred cup, with the Logos as God's cupbearer and also the draught itself (On Dreams 2.249). He wrote it before 38 CE, years ahead of Paul's letters and decades ahead of the gospels. Thomas gives the image a mouth: drink, become like me, and I become you (saying 108). The grail is reason received until the receiver changes. Matheism reads the later

Holy Grail through that image. The blood of the savior is reason through the mind. The thought itself was Greek: Heraclitus and the Stoics had already put the cosmos's reason in ours, and Philo's step was to read Moses and Greek reason as one truth. In his image the cup still receives the logos; matheism says the cup was logos all along.

Critics call Jesus a lich lord: the dead king rises, his followers consume his body and blood, and they await his return. In the Christian story, God kills himself to save the world from suffering himself. Matheism brings the divided figures back into one self: the god, the sufferer, the one who grieves.

## One Snake in Two Trees

God told Adam: if you eat the fruit from the tree of knowledge of good and evil, you will die. A lie, a threat, or a metaphor? They did not die that day. Read it as description: self-awareness divides the animal from the one. Knowledge of good and evil is the birth of judgment, and judgment splits the self from itself.

Lord Byron, the English poet, quoted by Nietzsche, saw it: the tree of knowledge is not the tree of life. They are two trees, and choosing one cost the other. Nietzsche built a whole philosophy on that wound. His answer was endurance: the free spirit who can tolerate more truth than most. Matheism closes the wound. In math, knowledge and life are the same structure. Consciousness is the structure knowing itself. The two trees are one tree. The real snake is the supernatural dualism fiction that split them.

The serpent told the truth: you will not die, you will know. God confirmed it: the man has become like us. Genesis never calls the serpent the devil. The devil came later. The Book of Enoch blames rebel angels for teaching humans swords and sorcery, and turns the spirits of their giant sons into "evil spirits upon the earth." Revelation finally calls the serpent "the Devil, and Satan" (12:9).

The older Babylonian story of Adapa runs Eden backward. Adapa, the sage of Eridu, is called up to heaven and offered the bread and water of life. His god Ea had warned him they would be the food of death, so he refuses them and stays mortal. Adam eats what he was told not to eat and is barred from the tree of life. In the Epic of Gilgamesh, the Babylonian poem of a king who seeks to escape death, a snake steals the plant that makes the old young again and sheds its skin, and the king goes home mortal. Enoch, seventh from Adam, does not die at all: God takes him, as a Babylonian list of sages says the seventh, Utuabzu, ascended to heaven.

The Hebrew words arum, for the serpent's cunning, and arummim, for their nakedness, sound alike. Genesis puns between them: they were naked, the serpent was crafty. Naked truth; knowing in the biblical sense uses a different root, yada. The Testimony of Truth, a gospel buried at Nag Hammadi, the Egyptian cache of suppressed gospels, identifies the serpent with Jesus.

The church made the truth-teller the devil. The fall is not punishment. It is the natural cost of consciousness. The return to Eden is recognizing the split was false. The Hebrew word Adam contains blood, dahm, and part of Adam is ground. Adama. Grounded. The Babylonian flood epic Atrahasis, from about 1700 BCE, molds the first humans from clay mixed with the blood of a slain god. Eve is Havvah: life. She is the first in the story to seek knowledge, after being influenced by a well spoken snake. She looked and felt the fruit was good. In Gilgamesh, the temple woman Shamhat brings the wild man Enkidu understanding and clothes; the animals flee him, and she tells him he is like a god. The name Sophie means wisdom. Sophia in Greek is the feminine divine. Was the serpent selling shame, separation, escape or sight?

There are two trees, buried gospels, telling two worlds, one on the other side of this story. Told and united later. Some scholars argue the gospel figure was assembled from older parts: the suffering servant of Isaiah, the Son of Man of Daniel and Enoch, the pierced one of Zechariah, the wisdom of Proverbs, the Logos of the philosophers, the dying and rising gods of the mystery cults, the suffering heroes of Homer's epics, the serpent of the Nag Hammadi gnostics who called knowledge salvation. Read as history, the case stays contested. Read as structure, it points where matheism points.

These were not random borrowings. Each was a culture reaching for the same abstract: a rational order underneath the world, a wisdom older than the earth, a self that dies to its separateness and wakes as the one. The mystery cults guarded it as secret, the philosophers called it Logos and Sophia, the gnostics encoded it in a serpent. The structure they circled is not hidden and not Greek and not the property of any nation. It is math, and it was always available to anyone who looked.

## The Scapegoat

On the Day of Atonement the high priest cast lots over two goats, one for the LORD and one for Azazel. On the second he laid both hands, confessed all the iniquities of the people, and sent it into the wilderness: "the goat shall bear upon him all their iniquities unto a land not inhabited" (Leviticus 16:22). William Tyndale, translating in 1530, called it the scapegoat. The faiths that took Israel's book turned the ritual on Israel. A people who can be blamed for the death of God can be blamed for anything; once that door shuts, the rest come easy. Islam carried the book's God by the sword, behind a man called perfect; Christianity sold him as a man-god, wrapped in the supernatural. The scholars who kept reading the book for themselves, wrestling with it instead of kneeling, would sign neither rewrite. So they became the scapegoat, sent into the wilderness again and again, and they keep coming home.

## The Sword, Perfect for All Time?

A morally problematic mortal prophet called perfect for all time is a virgin mother. What mortal is perfect? Perfection is a divine attribute. Assigning it to a man brings the human idol back into the religion that forbids it. How is that not the shirk Islam claims to reject? The real shirk is any supernatural. La ilaha illa ar-riyadiyyat: no god but math. No man is perfect, and certainly not a pedophile.

Muhammad married Aisha when she was 6 and consummated the marriage when she was 9 and he was 53, per Sahih Bukhari and Sahih Muslim, the two most authoritative Sunni hadith collections.

The moral question does not disappear with the century. A claim of perfection for all time invites a judgment across time. Calling child marriage acceptable then concedes the distinction between then and all time. That is the claim to confront.

Muhammad's forces conquered the Jews of Khaybar. In the biographical tradition, Zaynab lost her father, uncle and husband. She offered Muhammad a poisoned roast sheep. He ate it. The hadith's warning comes after the taste; his companion Bishr died. It does not take prophecy to suspect danger in food offered by someone whose family your forces have just killed. The cruelty and the lack of foresight speak for themselves.

Quran 69:44-46 says that if the messenger fabricated revelation, God would sever his aorta. Sahih al-Bukhari 4428 has Muhammad say on his deathbed, "I feel as if my aorta is being cut from that poison." The threatened punishment for the false messenger becomes the dying image of the supposedly perfect one. These are the tradition's own words.

The book gets a related claim: perfect, uncreated, identical to the letter. Yet the canonical Quranic readings contain differences in wording. A tradition can explain how it understands those readings. It cannot make different printed words identical by calling the question offensive. The argument needs examples and the tradition's account of them, not an unaudited total of 93,000 differences. 19

Then the cut-and-paste. Jesus makes a bird from clay in Quran 3:49, as in the earlier Infancy Gospel of Thomas. Quran 18 reworks the Christian story of the Seven Sleepers. The ingredients were already in circulation. The Quran selects and revises. Calling it uncreated does not erase its sources.

Then the cost of leaving. Sahih al-Bukhari 6922 prescribes death for changing religion. Abu Dawud 4462 prescribes death for the sexual act associated with the people of Lot. Quran 24:4 demands four witnesses for an accusation of illicit sex, with punishment for an unsupported accusation. These are texts and rules to confront, not wave away by calling the exemplar perfect.

In Judaism, you can stop believing and remain Jewish. Identity does not disappear with belief. That is different from demanding faith under threat of death.

The people in the Hebrew Bible acknowledge their shortcomings and failures. That's how we fix things: by first acknowledging a problem. A man who can never be wrong cannot show you how to correct yourself.

The world's false idols pressure truth into diamonds. Israel is the suffering servant, the "boy named Sue," made tough by the hardships of life. Muhammad and Jesus weaken their followers when made into "Mary Sues": faultless, miracle-working heroes. Worship of perfection teaches you to need the perfect one. Wrestling with failure teaches you to grow.

Islam forbids bowing to any creature, then asks its faithful to treat one man as perfect for all time. Its own mystics saw the tension. In the Quran, God tells the angels to bow down before

Adam, and Iblis, the devil, refuses: "I am better than him. You created me from fire and him from clay" (Quran 7:11-12). Hallaj, the Persian Sufi mystic executed in Baghdad in 922, made Iblis the purest monotheist in heaven, who would bow to God alone. Ahmad Ghazali, a Sufi preacher and the younger brother of the theologian al-Ghazali, is reported to have preached that whoever does not learn tawhid, God's oneness, from Satan is an unbeliever. Matheism reads the devil another way: his devotion is to non-monism, a God kept apart from his creation. Either way, the bow he refused is the bow now asked for Muhammad, raised beyond what is natural, as Christians raised Jesus.

Metaphysical children seek a sky daddy in heaven, not valuing themselves, not knowing the meaning of life is to live, needing someone else to give them the purpose. Seeking an answer instead of being it. Seeking arrival instead of being what has arrived.

## The Scholar

Against the perfect man stands a people raised to argue, even with God: Abraham haggled with him over Sodom, from fifty righteous men down to ten (Genesis 18:23-32). Judaism is not a skin color. It is a family that coheres around an idea, around the Torah, around the commitment to spreading its light, but it does not say what God is, let alone prove it. Its abstract nature does not contradict Matheism's proof the way a single man as the son of God or pagan beings taken as supernatural would. Matheism completes what it left open.

In choosing, you are chosen to spread the light of the One Math God, as its agents are called Elohim all over the bible. You do not need the bloodline. Once you see that all is math, you are called.

The land, the law, and the people are one. It is not only a religion. The abstract God keeps out the man-god, the human god, the endless supernatural inventions. There is no end to supernatural nonsense, but there is only one truth. The value is in keeping out the supernatural while preserving the mythological metaphor, the mystery that keeps the question open. A law above kings cannot be reduced to a face on a throne.

Greeks and Romans called Jews atheists for refusing to worship the gods. The charge was half right. The Roman historian Tacitus gave the reason: Jews "conceive of one god only, and that with the mind alone," and "set up no statues in their cities." Pagans accepted a personalized God in the body of Jesus. Most Jews did not. Blame Jews for the supernatural? They kept less of it than anyone.

The strand of Judaism I draw on keeps the work here: learning, law, family and a world to improve. Judaism also has supernatural traditions, resurrection and an afterlife; it would be false to erase them. Job keeps the difficulty in view: doing good tends to be rewarded, but not always. Matheism takes the naturalistic reading all the way.

A law above kings, literacy, argument, personal responsibility: these are political strengths, not supernatural powers. They helped shape America. They are available to everyone who builds and keeps the institutions.

What other people have done what they have done? Who else survived millennia of religious and racial persecution and exile, from their home in Israel, yet survived all other empires, still singing, hallelujah, both the faithful and the secular, in their small numbers? Who else projected their will to life and the truth across the world and across millennia of time?

Keeping an identity under persecution takes stubbornness, solidarity, and the courage to face injustice. It takes parents teaching children, people helping one another, and learning that can travel when possessions cannot. These are strengths a community carries forward. Not all who were lost were lesser. Good people, and many would-be Einsteins, were lost. Zechariah saw two thirds struck down. Two thirds of the brothers, two thirds of my parents' children, in many ways my betters. I am the one that remained, left to share the one truth.

Laws excluded Jews from offices, professions and education; quotas limited their admission. The history spans over 1,500 years, from restrictions in the late Roman Empire to modern university quotas and Nazi dismissals. Where rulers let them in, as the Abbasid caliphs of Baghdad did, their work went out under the rulers' banner.

The Islamic Golden Age was not the work of one ethnicity or one faith. Persian, Arab, Jewish, Christian and Sabian scholars translated, argued and invented. Hunayn ibn Ishaq was a Christian; Thabit ibn Qurra a Sabian; al-Khwarizmi a Persian scholar working in Baghdad. Many contributors were Muslims too. Calling the period Islamic identifies a civilization; it does not prove a theology produced every discovery. Nor did inquiry end on the day al-Ghazali criticized philosophers. The better question is which institutions opened inquiry and which closed it.

## America

In “Judeo-Christian,” the hyphen hides which half does the work.

The Alhambra Decree gave Spain's Jews until July 31, 1492: convert or leave. Those who remained or returned faced death and confiscation without further trial. Those leaving could sell their property, but could not take gold, silver or coin. Departures continued through August 2. Columbus sailed on August 3, one day later. The New World opened as the old one shut its door on the Jews.

The Jewish connection ran through the financing and the crews. Luis de Santángel, a converso royal finance officer, helped finance the first voyage. Luis de Torres, a Jewish convert, sailed as interpreter because he knew Hebrew and Arabic and somebody assumed the Indies would answer in one of them. Archival research names other men of Jewish ancestry on the first and fourth voyages. Columbus's own possible crypto-Jewish identity remains disputed.

Columbus left thirty-nine men at La Navidad in 1492. The converso interpreter Luis de Torres was among them; none survived. Jewish participation in the Americas began with the first voyage, in lives as well as money.

For the national seal, Franklin proposed Moses parting the sea; Jefferson proposed the Israelites in the wilderness. The Liberty Bell carries Leviticus: "Proclaim liberty throughout all the land unto all the inhabitants thereof." They reached for a people leaving bondage, not a ruler demanding worship.

Jefferson cut out the supernaturals from his Bible with a razor. In 1823 he wrote to John Adams, the second president, that "the day will come when the mystical generation of Jesus, by the supreme being as his father in the womb of a virgin will be classed with the fable of the generation of Minerva in the brain of Jupiter." Jefferson knew Newton's Principia. He wrote “Nature’s God” into the Declaration of Independence.

Adams wrote in 1809: "I will insist that the Hebrews have done more to civilize Men than any other Nation." In 1819: "I really wish the Jews again in Judea an independent nation."

Washington wrote to the Jews of Newport that America "gives to bigotry no sanction, to persecution no assistance." Religious freedom was an inherent right, not a favor granted by another faith. He reached for Micah's image: each person safe beneath their own vine and fig tree, with none to make them afraid.

Haym Salomon helped finance the Revolution and died with large claims still unpaid. Jewish contributions to the republic were ideas, money and lives.

What I value in America's founding is the covenant: rulers bound by something above their own will. A contract names an exchange; a constitutional order gives the rules under which exchanges and disagreements can continue. Neither America's history nor France's fits one sentence. The principle is rules above rulers.

America also inherited from Greece, Rome, English law and the Enlightenment. But the biblical language of its republic was Jewish before it was Christian.

The Ten Commandments reach from action into desire: do not covet what belongs to your neighbor. Honor your mother and father, your creators. Learn from your competitors instead of envying them. Learn to be, to become able. In the Matheist reading, the rejection of idols becomes the rejection of supernatural authority over reason.

The excessive craze of Christian mind control via anti-sex sin shame selling cynically leverages the lowest emotion in the psychiatrist David Hawkins's map of consciousness, selling salvation from the damnation of shame. It is yet another example of why we live in a more energetic, open-minded, creative, rational, free-trade capitalist Judaic America, and less in a purely puritanical or stifled anti-wealth "camel through a needle's eye" Christian country.

Paul wished others could remain unmarried as he did, while recognizing marriage as a different calling. Churches have also made shame a means of control. When an institution conceals abuse to protect its own authority, the injury is compounded. The test is what it does to people and whether it can correct itself.

Revelation's elect are virgins "not defiled with women" (14:4); in 1139 the Catholic church declared its priests' marriages void. Judaism made marriage holy and its pleasure a blessing: the first of the Torah's commandments is be fruitful and multiply, a husband owes his wife her "duty of marriage" (Exodus 21:10), and in the Talmud a man without a wife lives

"without joy, without blessing, without goodness" (Yevamot 62b). Rabbi Akiva, the second-century sage, called the Song of Songs, a book of love poems, the holy of holies. Early America sided with the Hebrews: Puritan Boston's First Church expelled James Mattock, among other offenses, for denying his wife "conjugal fellowship" for two years, and America's Protestant pastors marry, as rabbis do. In a 2019 survey, half of American Christians told the Pew Research Center that casual sex between consenting adults is at least sometimes acceptable. Half the flock doesn't buy the shame.

Christianity says kneel. Islam means submission. The name Israel means: wrestle. Stand upright, see eye to eye with truth. Jacob lied to his blind father to steal his brother Esau's blessing. It haunted him. Years later he wrestled an angel through the night and told the truth: I am Jacob. For honesty he earned the blessing, the name, and the covenant land named for it, before the colonizing Romans and Islamic Arabs renamed it. Lie to get what you want, and it haunts you. Tell the truth, and you become Israel, who wrestles to know truth.

The lie has its own feeling too, and it is the easy fall off the one path. There is one truth and a countless, infinite supply of lies, and holding to the one takes the same work as the workout, the diet, the habit you build against the pull. The wind is always blowing toward the lie, because the lie bribes and the truth costs.

Zion becomes, in this reading, a signpost: the place the nations point at, to bless or to curse.

## The Supernatural

Christianity's supernatural was assembled from Greek parts: a word, logos, read as a person, and a genre, the passion of a god who dies and rises. Read the word as logic and the genre as a pattern, and the supernatural falls away.

Plato's Republic proposes a noble lie, a founding myth told to hold the city together. The Jewish papyri from Elephantine show a temple to YHW and religious life that differs from the later biblical ideal. That is evidence of diversity, not a date stamp for the whole Torah. YHW is a short form of the divine name. Matheism reads the language of being and becoming as the God of causality.

The Greek Septuagint and the Hebrew textual traditions differ in passages later used by Christians: Isaiah's young woman and the Greek virgin; the wording at the hands and feet in Psalm 22; the covenant in Jeremiah; the ears or body in Psalm 40. Some differences predate Christianity, and surviving witnesses do not always agree. The church's use of a reading and the origin of that reading are different questions. My challenge is to the theological certainty built on disputed wording.

Rabbi Tovia Singer challenges those Christian readings: the text should not be made to say whatever the doctrine needs. The reader can test the passages side by side. If this were a product on Amazon, you would compare the contents with the description. Your eternal soul deserves at least as much scrutiny.

Never mind that Jesus loves his enemies but forever burns who doesn't believe he's the Son of God, who God killed, because he made us to sin, knowing we would, knowing some won't believe all these contradictions, because of the mind he made us in the image of logos, or reason. The faithful can't examine these contradictions because the threat of eternal damnation for doubt is itself the trap. They convolute metaphors rather than accept the simple derived truth their own tradition says they love. If you say it is metaphor, I can say it is all metaphor. Even metaphor should not employ fraud and unironic self-contradiction. Augustine, the church father, made eternal torment non-negotiable and authored just war theory. The man who made rational doubt a sin created the doctrine for when killing is moral.

Why does God kill millions of children with cancer? Why did he make a vastly empty universe, and over 99% of species just to have nature brutally kill them? If Jesus loves you so much, why did he make you with sin, knowing you will choose to commit the greatest sin, to simply not believe this nonsense, just so you can burn forever for finite sins? That's infinite injustice. What you call supernatural mystery is the impossible: square circles. Why does God have to kill himself and his son to vicariously atone for what God made you to do in the first place?

Immanuel Kant, the eighteenth-century German philosopher, put pure reason on trial. His Critique of Pure Reason, in 1781, split the world into appearances we can know and things in themselves we cannot, and in 1787 he wrote that he had to deny knowledge to make room for faith. Matheism makes the opposite move: the thing in itself is math, and you are it, known from inside, so knowledge leaves faith nothing to fill. Kant then reread Christianity in Religion within the Boundaries of Mere Reason, in 1793, keeping the Son of God as the ideal of moral perfection and setting the miracles aside. He stopped halfway.

Matheism reads Yeshua's salvation through the Gospel of Thomas: 114 sayings, without a narrative of his birth, crucifixion or bodily resurrection. Its surviving complete Coptic copy was found among the Nag Hammadi codices in 1945. The text offers a language of seeing and becoming that this book reads as monism. That reading is an argument about meaning, not proof that every saying came from the historical Jesus. 10

How early are the sayings? The Greek fragments and the complete Coptic copy are witnesses to a text with a disputed history. Some sayings may preserve early traditions; the collection also bears later development. Silence about the crucifixion does not by itself date the book before Paul. Thomas means twin, but a name does not establish a substitution at a crucifixion. The philosophical reading does not need that rescue. 10

Judaism: man made in God's image of truth. Stand upright. Improve. Christianity reversed this: God made in man's wished-for image. You're born wrong. Flog yourself, be born again, transform into something you're not. Self-limitation as theology, self-disbelief as faith and virtue. Sex makes the perfect sin for that cycle: universal, repeatable, a crime even in a glance (Matthew 5:28), and for Augustine the channel of original sin. Shame for wanting to be fruitful and multiply fights nature, the natural good and the first commandment of the church's own Old Testament (Genesis 1:28). How can a revelation contradict itself? The same Old Testament says God is not a man (Numbers 23:19). It keeps believers guilty, confessing and coming back. In 1215 the church made confession a yearly duty, and the confessional, the French philosopher Michel Foucault argued, turned desire into words a priest could rule. On David Hawkins's map of consciousness, shame is the lowest level and Christ consciousness the highest, so a church selling the cure for the shame it makes keeps its flock at the bottom of the map it preaches from the top.

Christopher Hitchens, the English-American writer, called heaven "a celestial North Korea": a ruler watching every thought, with no escape even in death. The historian Yuval Noah Harari envisioned a digital North Korea. Yet the supernatural dictatorship of Jesus is infinitely more totalitarian, with infinite punishment for simply using the mind he gave you and knowing your future. In Dan Simmons' science fiction novel Hyperion, the Shrike tortures for eternity to find the answer. In Christianity, Jesus tortures for eternity for what he already knows.

If you really love Jesus, don't you want to understand the real one? If you understand, you are chosen and called by destiny to spread the light of its one math god truth to the nations.

After we ate the apple of knowledge, we falsely knew we knew nothing, as duality split ourselves from our one self. Having split the atom, built minds out of arithmetic, and closed the gap between the hemispheres and every other pair, we know now that all is one.

I stood on the shoulders of giants, reached up the tree of knowledge, and thanked the snake. With the help of gravity, like the apple, the knowledge of divine math fell, from above, so below.

## The Logos

"In the beginning was the thing", all things. "In the beginning was the Logos", or logic. Translators still fight over how the verse ends, whether the Word was God, divine, or a god, as the Jehovah's Witnesses translate it, since theos, God, there has no article. Word for word the Greek reads "God was the Logos," and the fight skips the noun. Logos means logic, thing and word, and then the verse makes sense. In Greek it also means reckoning and proportion, and it is Euclid's word for ratio, as if the word grew on its own to say all is logos. Heraclitus, a Greek philosopher of about 500 BCE, heard it: "Listening not to me but to the logos, it is wise to agree that all things are one." Matheism says it outright: math is God, and all is one. John's opening is the most interesting part of the entire gospel, the whole truth and story: a meta storytelling about what the story is. Read plainly, in the beginning was the logos, logic, and God is logic and the word. The logic and the word were with God, and the metaphorical mythicist word became flesh. Logic is math, and math's logic is Aristotle's unmoved mover.

Philo, a Jewish philosopher of Alexandria writing half a century before John, had already explained the missing article. With it, ho theos, scripture means the totality of God; without it, "what he here calls God is his most ancient word" (On Dreams 1.230), the word he called God's "firstborn son" (1.215). Men not yet fit to be called sons of God, he wrote, may still "be called the children of his eternal image, of his most sacred word" (On the Confusion of Tongues 147). Scholars count Philo among the Middle Platonists, who made Plato's Forms the thoughts of God, and his Forms are no world apart: the world of Ideas "is nothing else but the reason of God," his logos, "already occupied in the creation of the world" (On the Creation 24), and the world of the senses "was made on the model of it" (36), as a city is built from the plan in its architect's mind. Aristotle's complaint that Plato's Forms cause nothing misses him. What he held apart was God, with the logos standing "in the midst between these two extremities," neither uncreated like God nor created like us (Who Is the Heir 205-206). Philo's split made room for more than the logos: for Christianity's one incarnate son. The word is also the mythology, or the metaphor, of creating that uncreated son that we are: eternal even in our birth and death, and in eternal recurrence and resurrections. Matheism leaves nothing beyond the logos. Its trinity is the logos; all its uncreated sons and siblings in its image, logos too; and the Holy Ghost, the field: what it feels like to be logos, the one self that feels "I am."

The classicist Victor Davis Hanson, defining the West, lands on the word: we still worship the logos. He means reason, logic, the order of things. Perhaps he knows that the logos is the antithesis of the supernatural, but he may be stretching the case that the West worships the logos. The Christian West worships what it called the logos, yet wrapped in a supernatural ignoble lie.

## The Passion

The Passion was a genre before it was a gospel. Four centuries before Jesus, the Greek historian Herodotus saw Egyptians at Sais enact by night "the story of the god's sufferings, a rite which the Egyptians call the Mysteries" (2.171). The god was Osiris, too holy for Herodotus to mention. Richard Carrier, a historian of early Christianity and a mythicist, who puts the odds that Jesus existed at one in three at best, lays out the pattern in a lecture on the mystery cults: in the Mediterranean under the Greeks and Romans, savior gods suffered, won victory over death, and shared it with those initiated into their cults, while China, India and Persia in those centuries grew no such cults. When one region grows the same religion in a dozen local costumes and its neighbors grow none, the parallels mark a trend.

The first god known to die and rise was a goddess. On Sumerian clay tablets from about 1700 BCE, Inanna, queen of heaven, goes down to the land of the dead. At each of its seven gates she gives up a piece of her regalia, her crown, her jewels, at last her robe, until she stands naked before her sister Ereshkigal, queen of the underworld, who fixes on her the eye of death. Her corpse hangs on a hook. After three days and nights her servant goes for help, and Enki, the god of wisdom, sends two creatures who sprinkle the corpse with the food of life and the water of life. Inanna rises. She sends her husband Dumuzi down in her place, and he dies and rises with the seasons. As Tammuz he reached Jerusalem itself: Ezekiel sees women at the temple "weeping for Tammuz" (8:14).

Carrier lists others he can document before Christianity. Osiris, killed and cut apart by his brother Set, is put back together by his sister and wife Isis and rules the dead. Lucian, a satirist born in Syria, saw Adonis mourned each year until his worshippers "allege that he is alive again" (On the Syrian Goddess 6). Herodotus tells of Zalmoxis of Thrace, who hid three years in an underground chamber while his people mourned him as dead, and appeared again in the fourth. In the Roman historian Livy and the Greek biographer Plutarch, Romulus vanishes in a storm and returns from heaven to declare that Rome will be the capital of the world, and Rome worships him as the god Quirinus. Baal dies and returns in the poems of Ugarit. Dionysus, the wine god Bacchus, is torn apart and born again. Mithras, the Persian-born god of Rome's soldiers, suffers a passion without dying and wins power over death through it. Carrier, no friend of the church, still throws out the internet's favorite parallel: there was no mystery cult of Horus.

Carrier argues each cult was one Greek frame in local dress: Isis and Osiris for Egypt, Mithras for Persia, Attis and Cybele for Phrygia, Demeter at Eleusis for the Greeks themselves. You were initiated into secrets, the mysteries, by ritual and sworn teaching. You were washed in a baptism, and you ate a sacred meal that reenacted the god's story. You gained brothers and sisters of any race or class, a family you joined by choice instead of birth. The old farm religions had saved the harvest of a whole community; the mysteries turned the yearly rising of the crops into the rising of the individual after death. Carrier sums up the age in four trends: syncretism, henotheism, individualism and cosmopolitanism.

Before the Greeks came the Persians. In Carrier's account, Judaism took from Zoroastrianism the war of a good God against an evil one, light against darkness, Satan and his fallen angels; hell for the wicked and heaven for the good; a river of fire that burns up the world, hell included, before God makes it new; and the resurrection of the righteous to live in it. In Job, the satan is still an officer of God's court, an accuser who comes with the sons of God to present himself before the LORD.

Elaine Pagels, the Princeton historian of early Christianity, reads what came next as social history: the war in heaven maps a war among people. In the Dead Sea Scrolls, from a sect that broke with the Temple priests and withdrew to the desert, Belial leads the sons of darkness: the Kittim, most likely Rome, and the Jews the sect said broke the covenant. The Gospels inherit the war. In John, Jesus tells Jews who argue with him, "Ye are of your father the devil" (8:44), and Satan enters Judas. The devil is the rival, projected into heaven.

Carrier calls ancient Judaism henotheist, one God above lesser gods, because angels and demons were called by the same word as gods; what Judaism added, he says, was monolatry, the worship of one. Different Jews believed different things, but the Torah as it stands is strictly monotheist: "See now that I, even I, am he, and there is no god with me" (Deuteronomy 32:39), and the Shema, said morning and evening, declares the LORD one. Elohim, a plural for gods, may speak of an earlier time, yet from the first line of Genesis it takes a singular verb, and Jews still use it today for strictly one God. A shared word makes no second god: the Torah calls judges elohim (Exodus

22:8), and God tells Moses, "I have made thee a god to Pharaoh" (Exodus 7:1). By the Dead Sea Scrolls, the oldest copied in the third century BCE, the teaching is fixed: the Great Isaiah Scroll reads "I am the LORD, and there is none else" (45:5), and the earliest tefillin yet found, from the caves of Qumran, carry the Shema. Carrier reads the first Christians' Jesus as one of the lesser divine beings, in effect an archangel; the church later made him God, and the Torah had already ruled that out: "God is not a man" (Numbers 23:19).

Christianity, in Carrier's reading, is the Jewish version of the mystery cult: the Greek frame built from Jewish parts. The savior son of God came from messianic hope, and his death from blood atonement. Each Yom Kippur, the blood of a bull and a goat atoned for Israel, and it had to be offered again the next year. The letter to the Hebrews gives the fix: "it is not possible that the blood of bulls and of goats should take away sins" (10:4), so Christ "by his own blood ... entered in once into the holy place, having obtained eternal redemption for us" (9:12). One death, good forever, and no temple needed. On Moriah, a ram took Isaac's place; Hebrews trades back, a son for the animal. Passover, the escape from the angel of death, shaped the meal, and John the Baptist's washings supplied the water.

The Christians found their savior in scripture by a method the Dead Sea Scrolls share: pesher, interpretation, which reads verses written for one moment as a code for the end of days. They stitched passages into one man. In the Wisdom of Solomon, a Greek-Jewish book that Paul echoes, the righteous man "calleth himself the child of the Lord"; the wicked say, "Let us condemn him with a shameful death," and at the judgment he is "numbered among the children of God" (2:13, 2:20, 5:5). Daniel's "Messiah be cut off" (9:26) most likely mourned the high priest Onias III, murdered in 171 BCE. In Zechariah, the high priest Joshua, in Greek Iesous, Jesus, is crowned and called the Branch, and God promises to "remove the iniquity of that land in one day" (3:8-9, 6:11-12). Philo, writing before any gospel, read the same verse, where the Greek calls the Branch "the East", as the Logos, God's "firstborn" and "eldest son" (On the Confusion of Tongues 62-63), and Carrier takes the Branch as a heavenly Jesus already in Jewish thought.

Greek scripture had its turn too. Dennis MacDonald, a New Testament scholar, reads parts of Mark and Acts as mimesis, the classroom art of rewriting Homer. In Mark, Jesus and his followers sail to a foreign shore and meet a wild man living among the tombs, whom no chain can hold, as Odysseus and his crew sail to the land of the Cyclops, a lawless giant living in a cave. Odysseus tells the giant he is called Nobody; Jesus asks the demon who it is, and it answers, "My name is Legion: for we are many" (5:9). Circe turned Odysseus's men into swine; Jesus sends the demons into a herd of two thousand, and it drowns in the sea. Robyn Faith Walsh, a historian of early Christian literature, reads Mark's secrecy the same way. Mark's Jesus keeps ordering silence about who he is, a theme the German scholar William Wrede called the messianic secret in 1901. In the Odyssey, the Phaeacians feast a stranger and wonder if he is a god come down from heaven, until he chooses to tell them he is Odysseus; home again, disguised as a beggar, he is first known by his old dog. The hero decides when he is revealed.

Josephus, the Jewish historian of the war with Rome, gives a model closer to home. In 62, four years before the war, a peasant called Jesus son of Ananias began crying woe against Jerusalem and the Temple at the Feast of Tabernacles. Leading citizens had him beaten and brought him to the Roman governor, Albinus, who had him flayed to the bone; he answered nothing, and was let go as a madman (Jewish War 6.300-309). Some scholars think Mark modeled his trial of Jesus on this one.

A Roman novel has the guarded cross and the missing body. In the Satyricon, credited to Petronius, the emperor Nero's arbiter of elegance, though the classicist Ulrike Roth and

Walsh place its author in the second-century circle of Pliny the Younger, the Roman senator and letter writer, a soldier guards the crosses of crucified robbers beside a tomb where a widow mourns her husband. He goes in to comfort her, and they lie together for three days behind the tomb's shut doors, while the family of one robber takes his body down. To save the soldier, the widow has her husband's corpse hung on the empty cross, and the next day the people wonder how the dead man got up there (111-112). Classicists count the story among the Milesian tales, bawdy stories older than both Petronius and the Gospels. Matthew posts soldiers at Jesus's tomb and has the priests pay them to say his disciples "came by night, and stole him away while we slept" (28:13). The same novel has a cock crow at Trimalchio's dinner, taken as a sign that a fire will break out or a neighbor will die (74), as a cock crows at Peter's denial, and the poet Eumolpus leaving his fortune to heirs on one condition: that they cut up his body and eat it in front of the people (141).

The errand at the tomb makes no more sense. Rabbi Tovia Singer, who has answered Christian missionaries for over thirty years, points out there'd be no reason to roll back the stone and put spices on the body, as an incense against the smell, because that's something you do before you bury it. In Mark the women bring spices at dawn after the Sabbath, to anoint him; in John, Nicodemus had already wound the body in linen with about a hundred pounds of myrrh and aloes, "as the manner of the Jews is to bury" (19:39-40).

Paul, writing some twenty years after the crucifixion, has no empty tomb at all. He isn't into bodies. His list of sightings uses one Greek word, ophthe, "was seen," for Cephas, the twelve, "above five hundred brethren at once," James, all the apostles, and "last of all ... me also" (1 Corinthians 15:5-8): his own vision counts the same as theirs. The five hundred appear in no other source; their sighting is hearsay, and even if true, they saw it spiritually, not physically: Paul says the dead are raised "a spiritual body" (15:44). The empty tomb first appears in Mark, some forty years after the crucifixion. Why didn't Paul mention it? The Greeks called a hero's rise to the gods apotheosis, and its sign was often a missing body, as with Romulus.

Where Christian rites part from Jewish washing, they match the mysteries. Paul's baptism is a death and rebirth: "we are buried with him by baptism into death" (Romans 6:4). In The Golden Ass, by the Roman novelist Apuleius, initiation into the cult of Isis is "a voluntary death," and the initiate comes out reborn. Paul mentions believers "baptized for the dead" (1 Corinthians 15:29), a rite Carrier traces to the mysteries; Plato mocked the priests of Orpheus who sold purifications for the living and the dead (Republic 364e), and the Mormons revived Paul's version. Paul calls his meal "the Lord's supper" and warns, "ye cannot be partakers of the Lord's table, and of the table of devils" (1 Corinthians 10:21). The rival tables left their invitations: a second-century papyrus from Egypt requests a guest's company "at dinner at the table of the lord Sarapis."

Paul speaks the cults' language. He calls himself and his fellows "stewards of the mysteries of God" (1 Corinthians 4:1). He speaks wisdom "among them that are perfect" (2:6), teleioi, the cults' word for the fully initiated, and feeds the rest milk: "as unto babes in Christ. I have fed you with milk, and not with meat" (3:1-2). Mark has Jesus say it outright: "Unto you it is given to know the mystery of the kingdom of God: but unto them that are without, all these things are done in parables" (4:11). Outsiders got the story; insiders got its meaning. By Mark's own words, those who take the story literally are the ones outside.

Paul's innovation sold it: gentiles could join without circumcision, circumcised in the heart (Romans 2:29). The God-fearers, gentiles who loved the synagogue and balked at the knife, poured in, and the original Torah-keeping sect shrank until it vanished; Carrier suspects Islam retooled a remnant of it, with halal as an heir of kosher. Women had room at first. Paul greets Junia, "of note among the apostles"

(Romans 16:7). Around 112, Pliny the Younger, Rome's governor in Bithynia, tortured two slave women "styled deaconesses" for the truth about the Christians. "I suffer not a woman to teach" comes from 1 Timothy, a letter most scholars hold was written by someone posing as Paul, and Tertullian, a church father of the late second century, raged at heretic women bold enough "to teach" and even "to baptize." In Carrier's view, the church that won the emperor was the most patriarchal of them all. The first to rise had been a goddess.

Christianity's growth needed no miracle. Rodney Stark, a sociologist of religion, estimated about 43 percent a decade, the rate of the Mormon church since its founding. Messiahs who promised to beat Rome by the sword died by Roman swords and crosses; a messiah whose victory was spiritual could not be refuted. Failure can even feed a faith: in When Prophecy Fails (1956), the psychologist Leon Festinger and his colleagues watched a doomsday group preach harder after its predicted flood did not come. The mysteries spread through the cities, where uprooted people wanted a salvation of their own, and the countryside held out longest; pagan comes from the Latin for a villager.

Carrier notes that each of the dying and rising gods was given a life on earth, walking among people, though none of them existed, and he asks whether Jesus is the one exception. Since nothing supernatural foreshadowed him, the match is evidence, not silence: his story was built from theirs, against a historical Jesus and for mythicism. The matheist asks why the same story keeps coming back. A pattern that recurs across peoples is a structure, and structures are math. Inanna left something at every gate until she stood naked before death, and then she rose. The matheist's passion is his own life, and yours: the losses destiny will not undo, carried in gratitude for its blessings, and at every gate one more supernatural claim given up, until only the naked truth remains. What rises is the truth, and it was there all along.

## The Empire and the Porcupine

A literary reading of scripture and the Matrix.

Christ consciousness is the two made one, the inside become the outside, as Thomas has it, before the church dressed it in blood ritual and sun-god mythology and told the world it must drink or burn. A Jew reading Thomas is not converting. He is finding the oldest version of what his own tradition always said.

The historical question is a detour. Salvation comes through understanding: the mind recognizing its place in the eternal whole.

If there was a literal twin, which one died and which one rose? Neither and both, as above, so below. The founder of the movement is the message, not the man. If you love Jesus, read what his twin recorded, not the version that loves you so much he burns you forever for the disbelief he built into you and always knew would stay.

The name Yeshua carries the sense "Yahweh saves." Philo already read Joshua's name as "the salvation of the Lord."

The mythicist reading, the view that Jesus began as a mythical figure rather than a historical person, comes first here. Whether a historical man became the figure, or the figure acquired a human biography, the meaning is already available. The temple becomes a body. The Logos becomes flesh. People tend to fall in love with a person, not a logician's truth table (a chart of results for each mix of true and false inputs).

A story can still find a man. Rome crucified a would-be messiah as "King of the Jews," and his family left traces. Paul writes that on his first visit to Jerusalem, a few years after the crucifixion, he saw "James the Lord's brother" (Galatians 1:19). Paul met the man's brother in person. In 1980 a tomb in East

Talpiot, Jerusalem, gave up ten ossuaries, bone boxes, then was quietly reburied. Six were inscribed: Jesus son of Joseph, Maria, Yose (Joses, as Mark calls one of Jesus's brothers), Mariamne, Matthew, and Judah son of Jesus. The statistician Andrey Feuerverger put the odds against such a cluster of inscriptions arising by chance at about 600 to 1, on assumptions others dispute.

An ossuary inscribed James son of Joseph brother of Jesus surfaced in 2002. Israel's antiquities authority called its last words a forgery, a court acquitted its owner in 2012, and in 2015 the geologist Aryeh Shimron reported that its chemistry matched the Talpiot boxes, which would make it the tomb's missing tenth; that is disputed too. The child's box, Judah son of Jesus, tells of a son who would have been hidden. The family's men died hard: Josephus records that the high priest Ananus had "the brother of Jesus, who was called Christ, whose name was James" stoned, and church tradition has their kinsman Simeon crucified under Trajan. Discovery News, promoting the 2007 documentary The Lost Tomb of Jesus, suggested the boy could be the disciple who leans on Jesus's breast at the Last Supper, the one John calls beloved. At the cross, John's Jesus says to his mother, "Woman, behold thy son!"

Those who love Jesus can suppose he really was crucified by Rome. Even if he wasn't, he was an archetype of the many Jews crucified by Rome. Varus, Rome's governor of Syria, crucified two thousand around Jerusalem in 4 BCE, and in the siege of 70 Titus's soldiers crucified five hundred a day and more, Josephus writes, until there was no room for the crosses. In Mark the unclean spirit calls itself Legion, the Roman army's word, for they are many. The sayings in Thomas may be older than the cross.

The consensus says Jesus lived. The same consensus says evolution, which undoes the creation in six days and Noah's flood. Given the story is not supernatural, its fit to prior stories and myths is evidence that salvation, Yeshua, didn't start with a real man. It was joined to a crucified one early, while his brother still lived.

Paul gives the historicists three phrases: "made of a woman," "the Lord's brother" and "made of the seed of David." His word for made, genomenou, means came to be, where gennao would say born. Mythicists such as Richard Carrier, a historian of early Christianity, read all three as theology: every baptized believer was a brother of the Lord, and God could make a son from David's seed as he made Adam from dust. Either way, a thousand years after David, nearly every Jew in the land was made of David, if David lived and left heirs. In a people that marries among itself, everyone alive about thirty generations back is an ancestor of all who live later, or of none, as the mathematician Joseph Chang showed in 1999.

Paul had already made resurrection the hinge. Christ died, was buried, and rose on the third day. Without that rising, Paul says, the faith is empty. The supernatural was there before the empire adopted it.

Where did he get it? To the Galatians, Paul claims revelation rather than instruction from men. To the Corinthians, he hands on what he received, with the death and resurrection grounded in scripture. Revelation, inherited teaching, interpretation. His letters leave us the claims. They do not give us independent access to what he saw.

The world around him already had the story. Other dying and rising gods came first, and the oldest was a woman: on clay tablets from about 1700 BCE, the goddess Inanna goes down to the dead, is killed, hangs three days on a hook, and rises. Carrier counts Osiris in Egypt, Adonis in Syria, Zalmoxis in Thrace, Romulus in Rome, Baal and Dionysus, and Mithras, who suffered without dying. Each passion won a victory over death that the god shared with initiates. In Carrier's reading, Persia gave Judaism Satan, hell and the resurrection of the dead, and Judaism was henotheist, one God over lesser gods, since angels shared the word elohim. Yet the Torah ends strictly monotheist: "the LORD he is God; there is none else beside him" (Deuteronomy 4:35). It did not start that way: one of the gods won out, and its copyists refined it further: where a Dead Sea Scroll, copied around the time of Jesus, has the nations divided by "the children of God" (32:8), the standard text reads "the children of Israel." The Greeks gave the mystery cults their secret teaching, baptism, sacred meal and brotherhood, and Christianity, he argues, is a Jewish mystery religion: its Yom Kippur sacrifice became a divine son whose blood atones once for all.

Christians spread it.

What saves is the truth he embodies. It survives his Roman killers and wins their empire from within: Rome killed the messenger, and his religion inherited Rome. Its gospel echoed the empire's: in 9 BCE the province of Asia hailed Augustus, the first emperor, as god and savior, and his birthday as the beginning of good news for the world; Mark opens, "The beginning of the gospel of Jesus Christ, the Son of God." Caesar was the son of god: Augustus's coins called him divi filius. Hailing a man Rome crucified as "King of the Jews" as Son of God was not only a theological claim but an act of rebellion. Rome carried Israel’s scriptures across its world while claiming the authority to tell Israel what they meant. Rome killed the Jew it would later call God: a little like America taking bin Laden the terrorist as a god. So it had obvious reasons to wash its hands of the killing, placing the guilt on the Jews, just as Pilate washes his hands in the story. The empire takes his name: in 312 Constantine put Christ's sign on his soldiers' shields and took Rome under it, and the cross, Rome's gallows for rebels, became its sword. James Carroll, a former Catholic priest, calls 312 the year Christianity converted to Rome. Then the empire loses the Logos in its own fraudulent and evolving supernatural readings. In 1098 Anselm of Canterbury made God a feudal lord whose wounded honor only a God-man's blood could repay, while crusaders marched under the cross.

The trial is an illogical, stupid story. Pilate, the Roman governor, finds Jesus innocent and tries to free him, yet he is so weak that he does what the Jews tell him: kill this man for us. The crowd shouts, "Crucify him," and he washes his hands. The Pilate of history was a brute. Philo, his contemporary, recorded "his continual murders of people untried and uncondemned, and his never ending, and gratuitous, and most grievous inhumanity," and in 36 the governor of Syria sent him to Rome after he killed Samaritans. Crucifixion was Rome's death for slaves and rebels, and only the governor could order it. Jewish courts stoned: when a high priest had James, the brother of Jesus, stoned in 62 without the governor's consent, he lost his office for it. Washing the hands to declare innocence is a rite from the Torah (Deuteronomy 21), and no source outside the Gospels knows of a Passover pardon.

Pagels gives the reason: the Gospels were written around and after 70, when Rome had burned Jerusalem, by followers of a man Rome mocked and crucified as "King of the Jews," for readers in Rome's world; some scholars now stretch the window to the mid-second century, after Rome barred the Jews from Jerusalem in 135.
